<image-desc>The logo for Google in its classic multi-colored serif font.</image-desc>

This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world's books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the <term>copyright: The legal right that grants the creator of a work exclusive control over its use and distribution.</term> to expire and the book to enter the <term>public domain: Creative works that are no longer protected by intellectual property laws, allowing the public to use them freely.</term> A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that's often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other <term>marginalia: Handwritten notes or marks made in the margins of a book by previous readers.</term> present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book's long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to <term>digitize: The process of converting physical text or images into a digital format that can be processed by a computer.</term> public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

* **+** *Make non-commercial use of the files* We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.
* **+** *Refrain from automated querying* Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google's system: If you are conducting research on <term>machine translation: Using software to translate text from one language to another.</term>, <term>optical character recognition: (OCR) Technology that recognizes printed or written text characters inside images and converts them into machine-encoded text.</term> or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.
* **+** *Maintain attribution* The Google "watermark" <note>A faint, identifying image or text placed on a document to identify its origin.</note> you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.
* **+** *Keep it legal* Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can't offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book's appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. <term>Copyright infringement: Using or reproducing copyrighted material without permission in a way that violates the owner's exclusive rights.</term> liability can be quite severe.

## About Google Book Search

Google's mission is to organize the world's information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world's books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web at <insert>http://books.google.com/</insert>

### CONCERNING THE
# LITERATURE
### OF THE
# IRISH NATION
### IN
## HEATHENISH TIMES.

Amongst all the proofs that can be exhibited of the existence of letters and learning in pagan times, no one can be so much depended on, as the producing of certain works, that were really written by pagan authors, and which existed before St. Patrick’s arrival in this island: Such a proof would doubtless solve the question proposed, in a decisive manner. The <term>poems</term> <note>original: "dans"; from the Irish word *dán*, meaning a poem, craft, or gift.</note> given us by Keating <note>Geoffrey Keating (c. 1569–c. 1644) was a prominent Irish historian; his work sought to defend the antiquity and culture of the Irish people.</note> to attest the histories of remote times, far from assisting us on the occasion, rather have appeared to many as proofs against us, because the words, the style, and the very poetic dress of these poems, show them to be of modern date and invention. Most of that historian’s poetic quotations regarding the <term>Belgian</term> <note>A reference to the *Fir Bolg*, one of the legendary groups of early settlers in Irish mythology.</note> and <term>Danian</term> <note>Refers to the *Tuatha Dé Danann*, a supernatural race in Irish mythology said to have ruled Ireland before the arrival of the Milesians.</note> colonies, the <term>Stone of Destiny</term> <note>original: "lia fail"; the ancient coronation stone located at the Hill of Tara.</note>, etc. are taken from poems that, by the *book of Lecan* <note>The *Book of Lecan* is a major medieval Irish manuscript created in the late 14th and early 15th centuries, containing genealogical, historical, and hagiographic material.</note>, are attri-

<summary>This page introduces an investigation into pre-Christian Irish literacy, questioning the authenticity of ancient poems cited by the historian Geoffrey Keating. The author suggests that while Keating uses these works as historical evidence, their modern style often makes skeptics doubt their pagan origins.</summary>

<keywords>Irish literature, pagan Ireland, Geoffrey Keating, Book of Lecan, Fir Bolg, Tuatha Dé Danann, Lia Fáil, St. Patrick, historiography</keywords>

# LITERATURE OF THE IRISH

buted to *Eochaidh Ó Floinn* <note>original: "Ecoba o Floin"; a 10th-century Irish poet and historian known for his chronological poems on the early migrations to Ireland.</note>, who was a Christian poet. From the discovery of this truth, which neither Keating <note>Geoffrey Keating (c. 1569–1644), author of "Foras Feasa ar Éirinn" (Foundation of Knowledge on Ireland).</note> nor any of our scholars did take proper care to explain, the enemies of our ancient history claimed victory, saying that these ancient facts had only modern writers for their first inventors; but let them proceed with less haste, and consider the true nature of things. The Irish Christians when they began to compose verses, after the manner of the *deibhidhe* <term>deibhidhe</term> <note>A common Irish syllabic poetic meter characterized by a specific rhyme between a stressed and unstressed syllable.</note> or *dán díreach* <term>dán díreach</term> <note>The "strict" or "direct" verse; the most formal and complex style of classical Irish poetry used by professional bards.</note>, which of all others existing in either our own or any other language I am acquainted with, was the most concise, and for this reason the most proper for writing history, applied themselves to recording the facts of remote antiquity, as well as chronology, etc. in such kind of verses.

And thus the Christians had, in an abridged manner, laid before them the lives and memorable actions of the princes, the years of their reign, etc. and hence Keating or any other writer may very securely cite one of these ancient Christian poets, for attesting facts belonging to pagan times, just as he might cite the same poet for attesting facts belonging to the Assyrian, Median, Persian, or Roman empires; the principal revolutions of all which states have been often faithfully recorded by these Irish poets, and many are to be met with in the parchment manuscripts still existing. The truth is, it was much easier to cite a verse of four lines, each line consisting only of eight syllables, wherein three or four facts may be recorded, than to have the trouble of citing an old passage in prose, such as it was before Christianity, which on account of its obscurity and uncouth...

<summary>The author defends the use of medieval Christian poetry as a reliable source for pagan Irish history, arguing that these poets used concise verse forms to preserve much older records. He compares the Irish preservation of their own history to their recording of the histories of the great ancient world empires.</summary>

<keywords>Eochaidh Ó Floinn, Geoffrey Keating, deibhidhe, dán díreach, Irish historiography, oral tradition, syllabic poetry, ancient empires</keywords>

# IN HEATHENISH TIMES.

uncouth dress <note>The author uses "uncouth dress" as a metaphor for the archaic or unrefined style of ancient Irish verse, which might seem unattractive to 18th-century readers who preferred a more polished "Enlightenment" style.</note>, must become disagreeable to the modern reader, who sought for elegancy of style, plain language and pleasure in his reading. And hence, as it would be highly contradictory to all good reason, for a man to conclude, that a fact or revolution, happening in any of the aforesaid great empires, had for its only authority an Irish Christian poet, because such a fact or revolution was really described by him, so in like manner it is repugnant to good sense, that a Christian poet should be assigned as the only author for facts which happened in heathenish times <note>Refers to the pre-Christian, pagan era of Irish history.</note>, because these facts are recorded in his verses; the reason he records in his poetry the facts relating to those empires of the world, is, because he read the history of them, and understood the language wherein the facts were contained, and by a like reason doth he record in verse, the facts regarding his own country, because he is well acquainted with the history of it, and understands his native language to perfection.

In my humble opinion the verses of such poets deserve the greatest credit, since they lay before us the most absurd relations and ridiculous tenets of our pagan ancestors, as well as the most certain and well connected points of their doctrine and history. This could not proceed from a want of discernment and good sense in them, since they show by other writings, they were well acquainted with foreign history, poetry and oratory, and consequently had as good a right as we have to distinguish between palpable error and truth, or plausibility; and since they relate these palpable errors and fabulous story, it appears, their true motive

for

<summary>The author defends the reliability of medieval Irish Christian poets as historians, arguing that they recorded ancient pagan traditions with the same scholarly intent they applied to Roman or Persian history. He suggests that the inclusion of "absurd" pagan myths is actually proof of their honesty in preserving original sources rather than a lack of intelligence.</summary>

<keywords>Irish Christian poets, historiography, pagan ancestors, historical reliability, Irish history, oral tradition, medieval scholars</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument from the previous section, asserting that Christian poets in Ireland acted as faithful historians of the pagan era. It transitions from discussing historical verse to presenting physical evidence—ancient legal fragments—to prove that literacy and written law existed in Ireland prior to the arrival of Christianity.</meta>

### LITERATURE OF THE IRISH

...for so doing <note>The author is referring to the Christian poets' decision to record ancient myths and history exactly as they found them.</note> was to hand down these accounts, just as they found them in our pagan history, with sincerity and candor. The Christian religion derives one advantage from their fidelity—namely, that we can by that means take a retrograde view <note>A backward-looking or retrospective look at the past.</note> of pagan blindness and misery.

Having thus offered my thoughts in vindication <note>In defense or justification.</note> of these verses, which I suppose to have been composed by Christian poets, celebrated in their times (for I know what little dependence ought to be had on the versification of our modern bards), I shall now proceed to give some clear proofs of our having had the use of letters <note>This refers to a written alphabet and literacy.</note> before Christianity. The proof I speak of is the exhibition of a fragment of a Code of Laws <note>The author is likely describing a manuscript of the "Brehon Laws," the ancient legal system of Ireland.</note>, which, I think, every judicious reader, who is versed in Irish antiquities, will judge to have been the product of very remote times, much higher <note>Meaning significantly older or further back in time.</note> than the fifth or sixth centuries.

This fragment contains twelve folios <note>A folio is a leaf of a manuscript, consisting of two pages (front and back).</note>, and begins at folio sixty-four; the sixty-three preceding were missing. And here I must beg the reader to remark the difference between the letter of the law and the explication of the said law. I maintain the first is of pagan institution, and that the second is the work of one or many Christian <term>Jurisconsults<gloss>legal experts who interpreted and applied the law</gloss></term>, who interpreted this law and applied it to particular cases, according to the intention of the Christian legislator.

This collection was partly made in the time of Donal mac Aoda mac Anmirech <note>Domnall mac Áeda (died c. 642 AD), a High King of Ireland.</note>, who was a Christian prince, by Ceandfaela mac Aillil <note>Cenn Fáelad mac Aillila (died c. 679 AD), a scholar known as "the Learned" who survived a head wound and was said to have a miraculous memory.</note>, called I suppose *Ceandfaela na fogluma* <note>original: "Ceandfaela na fogluma" — Cenn Fáelad of the learning (the Learned).</note>; and partly by Aicill <note>This refers to the *Book of Aicill*, a major text of the Brehon Law. Aicill is also a hill near the Hill of Tara.</note> who lived after Ceandfaela’s time (on the environs of Tara) and after the time of king Cormac <note>Cormac mac Airt, a legendary High King of Ireland from the 2nd or 3rd century.</note>, for he

<meta>catchword: was</meta>

<summary>The author argues that Christian Irish poets preserved pagan history accurately and presents a twelve-folio fragment of ancient law as evidence that Ireland possessed written language long before the Christian era.</summary>

### IN HEATHENISH TIMES.

<meta>catchword: Plebiscita</meta>

...was a contemporary with *Cairbre Lifechar*, <note>Also known as Cairbre of the Liffey, a High King of Ireland in the 3rd century.</note> son of *Cormac*. Nevertheless, the work is attributed to *Cormac*, as it was begun by him and finished during the reign of his son *Cairbre*. Thus, the Irish lawyers who compiled and delivered this code of laws <note>The author is referring to the "Brehon Laws," the native legal system of Ireland that operated for centuries before being replaced by English common law.</note> were *Aicill*, <note>While the author treats "Aicill" as a person here, historical scholarship usually identifies Aicill as a place name—specifically the Hill of Skryne—where King Cormac resided while writing the laws.</note> who lived in the reigns of *Cormac* and of his son *Cairbre*, and *Cenn Fáelad*, <note>Cenn Fáelad mac Aillila (died c. 679), a famous scholar credited with recording many Irish laws and genealogies after suffering a head wound that supposedly gave him a "brain of forgetting" (a prodigious memory).</note> son of *Aillil*, who flourished in the reign of *Domnall*, son of *Áed mac Ainmuirech*. 

There is no doubt that these laws were collected by the order of those Irish princes, just as we know the Roman emperor *Justinian I*—who had the Roman laws collected through the care and labor of ten able legal experts, directed in chief by the celebrated *Tribonian*—called this collection the *Code of Justinian*. He also caused the scattered decisions of his judges and magistrates, reduced to the number of fifty, to be called the *Digests* <note>original: "digesta"</note> or *Pandects*, <note>original: "pandectæ"</note> to all of which he added his four books of *Institutes*, which are an abridgment of the text of all the Roman laws; and lastly he published his own laws which he called *Novels* <note>original: "novellæ" — specifically the "New Constitutions" issued after the initial code.</note>; this compilation has secured an immortal name for that emperor.

Our Irish Christian legislators took the same method as the Christian legal expert *Tribonian* and his illustrious assistants called *predecessors* <note>original: "antecessores"</note> or professors and interpreters. In the preface to the *Pandects*, they are styled "teachers of the laws" <note>original: "των νόμων διδασκάλοις"</note>—I mean *Theophilus* and *Dorotheus*, who explained not only the laws of the Christian emperors from the year 527 (when *Justinian* succeeded his uncle *Justin* in the empire) up to the reign of *Constantine the Great*, but also back to the times of *Julius* and *Augustus Caesar*. They even introduce the laws of the senate and the

<summary>The author compares the compilation of ancient Irish Brehon laws to the famous codification of Roman law under Emperor Justinian. He argues that Irish scholar-lawyers like Cenn Fáelad acted with the same systematic rigor as the Roman jurists Tribonian, Theophilus, and Dorotheus.</summary>

<keywords>Brehon Law, Cormac mac Airt, Cairbre Lifechar, Cenn Fáelad, Justinian Code, Tribonian, Pandects, Roman Law, legal history, Irish antiquity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the legal reforms of the Irish princes, comparing their efforts to those of the Roman Emperor Justinian. The previous page ended while describing the Roman legal collections, the Digests and Pandects.</meta>

### FRAGMENT OF THE [LAWS]

Plebiscites <note>Laws or decisions passed by the common people (the plebs) in ancient Rome.</note> or decisions of the popular tribunes, and sometimes the Laws of the Twelve Tables <note>The earliest code of Roman law, dating to the 5th century BCE.</note>; all which were of pagan institution, though directed and compiled by a Christian emperor and Christian legal experts <term>Jurisconsults: scholars or experts in the law.</term>

These Irish princes, seeing the laws of the Irish nation dispersed in many pieces, thought it expedient, both for the public order and economy of the state, and for the safety and comfort of each individual, to unite them all together in one code.

The FRAGMENT begins by the invocation of the name of God thus: *This is in the name of GOD* <note>original: "in ainm DE ſo"</note>; just like the Justinian code, which begins *In the name of our Lord Jesus Christ* <note>original: "in nomine D. noſtri Jeſu Chriſti"</note>; and the first leaf <note>original: "folio"</note> contains an historical prelude to the law, as I have just now observed, namely:

The place of this book
was Derry-loran, and the time
was the time of Domnall,
son of Áed, son of Ainmire;
and the person of it <note>Meaning the author or scribe.</note>
was Cenn Fáelad, son of Ailill.
<note>original: "Locc don liubarſa Daire lubran, agus aimfer, do aimſer Domnall mac Aeda mac Ainmirech, acus pearſa do, Ceandfaelamac Aillila."</note>

The next page begins a kind of introduction to the work in this way: "The temptation of the serpent <note>transcribed as *athair* (father), but likely *nathair* (serpent)</note> upon Eve and Eve’s consent to it" <note>original Irish: *aslach on athair for Ebha & toltnugad do Ebba fria*</note>, that is, the serpent presented the forbidden fruit to Eve, and Eve consented to receive it. "Transgression" <note>original: *Imarbas*</note> is the prohibition of a legislator. "Eve's delivery of it to Adam in disobedience to the Lord" <note>original: *Com fugud do Adam fria flatarta um coimde*</note>, meaning Eve delivered it to Adam in disobedience to the Trinity; and hence it is plain, says the author, that when a man knows a crime in virtue of the law established, though he be ignorant of the penalty enjoined by it, he may be punished according to the full measure.

"And from this it is evident that when a person has knowledge of the crime, even if they do not know the [legal] lie <note>original: *na berce*, referring to the specific penalty or legal definition</note>, they shall pay the full penalty" <note>original Irish: *is as sin is follos o bias fis in cinaid ac duine gen coraib fis na berce con adhainfis lanfrach*</note>. Hence, by a greater right, when the crime or fault is known and the penalty too, there is no cause for deception <note>original: *ni damna meallaid*</note>.

**THE DIVISIONS OF UNINTENTIONAL CRIME <note>original Irish: *EISLIR FODLA EITGID*</note>. How many are there, or in what number of divisions is the comprehensive crime of the offender divided**; i.e. how many fold and how extensive is the crime and how many ways it can be distinguished.

**IT IS NOT DIFFICULT <note>original Irish: *NIHANDSUSON* — a standard formula in legal texts used to introduce an explanation</note>,** means that whatever is done by advice has the same force with the act itself.

**THE FOUR, OR THE FOURFOLD NUMBER SURPASSES ALL OTHER NUMBERS <note>original Irish: *ACEATHAIR, NO DOIRISCE AN AIREAM CEATARDA PONA HUILIB AIRMHE*</note>.** The number four excels all other numbers on account of the elements, of the four cardinal points, and for many other reasons, so that the proof of four persons is beyond dispute; the interpretation is in Latin thus, **"the number four excels all numbers in the universe"** <note>original Latin: *quaternus præcellit omnibus numeris in ella*</note>; i. e. the universe (a).

(a) Here we may take notice, that the Irish glossary adopts the allegorical method of reasoning, of which the Platonic philosophers were extremely fond. Cornutus the Stoic <note>A philosopher from the 1st century AD</note> has left us a treatise on allegorical interpretations, from which, and from his study of Platonic philosophy, he is thought to have borrowed his method of allegorizing several passages of holy writ <note>The Bible</note>. See Porphyrius as quoted by Eusebius in *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 6, Chapter 19. Many of the early Church fathers fell into the same method; St. Irenæus reasons much after the same manner as our Irishman on the number of four evangelists; he says, "there must be four gospels, and no more, from the four winds and four corners of the earth." As that kind of reasoning was acceptable to the learned Gentiles,

### BREHON LAWS

**DELIBERATE PRESENCE <note>original Irish: *COMRAITE*</note>**, means the physical presence of a person, which is to be identified by the senses of the body or the five senses <note>original Irish: *ceadfaid cuirp no 5 sianfib*</note> that agree and join together in the committing of a crime; it may concern the witness, whose mere statement that he heard the fact will not be enough. The Biblical story of **Susannah** <note>A reference to the Book of Daniel, where Susannah is falsely accused by elders; she is saved when the prophet Daniel exposes their conflicting testimonies, highlighting the need for consistent witnesses.</note> reminds us of this necessary precaution. The concealment of the crime <note>original Irish: *cleithe an cinaid*</note> should also be considered, as well as the proper way to determine which of the two competing parties made their declaration first <note>original Irish: *coir ciamad don las-bud mo ro aisned artus*</note>.

**THE UNWARY MAN, or THE ONE REMOVED FROM YOU, or THE IGNORANT MAN <note>original Irish: *ACASANFOT, or AMBI UAIT, or AINEOLACH UAIT*</note>**, means that an ignorant man, who is like a beast, should not be permitted to testify to anything, for thus says Fearmuvan son of Echlan:

| It is a riddle commonly encountered by the young <note>original Irish: *is brecht ciamtiagait oig*</note> | signification of **foit** <term>foit: an archaic legal term for a wary or sensible person</term>; it is a riddle commonly proposed to youth. |
| I say that *fot* means sensible <note>original Irish: *coria de ata fot faiteach*</note> | I say that **fot** signifies sensible and **anfhot**, foolish, |
| and *anfhot* means foolish <note>original Irish: *agus anfhot anfaitech*</note> | nonsensical. |

**EXEMPTION <note>original Irish: *TORBA* or *TOIRBEO*</note>**, means the sentence of a judge which frees the criminal from further fears of arrest after he has paid the fine. Some are of the opinion it means shame <note>original Irish: *tair*</note>, confusion, or else terror to him who does not pay for the damages

### FRAGMENT OF THE BREHON LAWS

...he has done; the debt is paid to the living person after the commission of an offense <note>original Irish: *tairuge don beo ris na hictar feic iar ndenam fogla*</note>.

**Noble is the living <note>original Irish: **Acasesba**, interpreted here as **Uais beo**</note>**, that is, happy is he who suffers a loss unjustly; it is applied in this way: Blessed is the man who seeks no compensation from those who offend him, and blessed is he who makes satisfaction after having committed a crime <note>original Irish: *icas iar denamfogla*</note>. This term is also used to signify the full number of witnesses required by law for certain crimes; for example, in cases of murder or robbery <note>original Irish: *fagla*</note> the number four is required, yet in some cases, two may be sufficient, particularly in cases of **deliberate injury** <note>original Irish: *etgen comraite*</note> and **accidental damage** <note>original Irish: *torba*</note>.

->Of the **partner in crime** <note>original Irish: *fear compairte*</note> and the **friend**, from the fourth<-
->division, which comes under the category of **Restitution** <note>original Irish: **Etgin**</note>.<-

**What are the names of those crimes?** or **The classification of the names of crime** <note>original Irish: **Ci an anmand na cina sin**, or **fodail anamand cinaid**</note>. What is the true name of the crime? It is the act itself which constitutes the crime <note>original Irish: *isi cin and in gnimugad*</note>, though a crime is also a form of restitution <note>original Irish: *etgen*</note>. There are four divisions of restitution, and ten specific types of restitution.

**Neglect is not difficult to explain** <note>original Irish: **Ni handsa on faill**</note>, or **neglect results in the penalty of sick-maintenance** <note>original Irish: **do fairgeand afaill smeacht heatha othrusa**</note>, which means that if a man be wounded in the body through malice, the aggressor must ensure the wound is healed <note>original Irish: *as faill do gan an totbrus do denam* — literally: it is neglect for him not to perform the sick-maintenance</note>.

**Neglect** <note>original Irish: *Eisleis*</note> or **failure of duty** <note>original Irish: *ailse leasa*</note>, refers to a crime committed with a full and malicious intent; the term is derived from "knowledge with him" <note>original Irish: *fis leis*</note> or from the failure to provide a benefit.

**Intentional Harm <note>original Irish: **Elgnas**</note>** is the foreknowledge of the thing being done or the act of doing it, whereby a person receives a wound or damage.

It emphasizes the "sick-maintenance" (othrusa) system, where an attacker is legally bound to provide for the victim's recovery.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the glossary of Irish legal terms, moving from the general nature of crimes and restitution discussed on page 16 to specific definitions of intent, negligence, and the physical circumstances of a legal offense.</meta>

# BREHON LAWS

**Anfis** means ignorance; such, for example, as the "blind casts" (*dall urchur* <note>original Irish: *dall urchur*, referring to a shot or throw made without a clear line of sight</note>), namely: throwing over a house, not knowing who is on the other side; through a wood when in full leaf; and through a field of standing corn; these are the three **blind casts**: *over a house in its covering; through a wood of leaves; over a field of corn.* <note>original Irish: *tar tic fo tuide; dar fid do duille; tar gort airbha*</note>

**Imraichne** means one out of his natural senses <term>imraichne: an error of identification or a person in a confused state of mind</term>, *a person in an altered or unlawful state.* <note>original Irish: *fear richta in ecose dilsig*</note> 1. *it refers to a failure of recognition.* <note>original Irish: *ime do merad aithne*</note>

**Tucaid** or **togairm**, signifies a process in law <term>tucaid/togairm: the legal cause or summons that initiates a case</term>.

**Turtait** or **toirteit**, is a place where the trespass is done.

**Tidradus**, is a perfect proof of the fact, called *the liability of the driving.* <note>original Irish: *cin na himana*</note>

**Fochuin**, is a crime committed in anger or passion <term>fochuin: a secondary cause or an indirect motive for an act</term>.

**Acais**, is the proximity of the place <term>acais: the primary or immediate cause of an event</term>.

**Agh**, is the act or perpetration of murder.

**Deabaid**, is debate; each debate is to be weighed according to the *cin* or crime, and the penalty proportioned thereunto. *To be fulfilled completely as is proper according to justice for every crime.* <note>original Irish: *A comflanug amail as comtechta do reir coir la gac cin*</note>

**Three divisions of trespass** <note>original Irish: *Teora fodla fogla*</note>, that is, **trespass** <term>fogail: injury, damage, or legal violation</term> is three-fold, and may be committed in three manners, namely: by the heart, by the tongue, and by the act itself; to these correspond the three terms:
1st. **Elguin** <term>elguin: malice aforethought or deliberate intent to cause harm</term>, when it regards **deliberate intent** <note>original Irish: *comraite*</note> and determines the species of the crime.
2d. **Anfot** <term>anfot: negligence, carelessness, or inadvertence</term>, when it determines the species and is referred to **deliberate intent**.
3d. **Toifced** <term>toifced: an accidental occurrence or sudden necessity</term>, when it regards neither the genus nor the species, but is applied to **accidental damage** or **useful purpose**. <note>original Irish: *Torba*</note>

**Malice is over anger, and anger is upon it; anger is against malice** <note>original Irish: *Elguin for, ferg agus is fuirre ferg fri Elguin*</note>, namely: **Malice** is over *anger* and *anger* is under **malice**; for without **malice** there is never anger.

<summary>This page provides a glossary of technical Brehon Law terms defining various levels of legal responsibility, ranging from "blind casts" (accidental harm) to Elguin (premeditated malice). It categorizes crimes by the intent of the perpetrator and the physical circumstances of the act.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the glossary of Irish legal terms from page 16, moving from physical acts and proximity to the psychological states and legal logic governing intent, negligence, and desire.</meta>

# FRAGMENT OF THE BREHON LAWS

and all anger is *Malice* <term>Elguin: often translated as "malice" or "deliberate intent" in a legal context</term>; for a man does many things through *Malice* which are not of anger, and does nothing through anger which is not of *Malice*, therefore *Malice* is a more extensive term than anger or *ferg* <note>original Irish: *ferg*</note> (b). *Not every malice is anger, though every anger is malice; a man may do many things through malice that are not from anger, but he will do nothing through anger that is not also through malice; thus, malice is broader than anger.* <note>original Irish: "Ni can elguin is ferg is gach ferg is elguin; do gena duine moran tre elguin nad bi ferg, agus ni digne tri ferg ni na bi helguin; is fairleithe elguin ina ferg defide."</note> ●

**Negligence over Idleness. i. e. Negligence is over Idleness,** <term>Anfot for Espa: "Anfot" refers to inadvertence or negligence; "Espa" refers to idleness or horseplay</term> i. e. *negligence* is greater than or over *idleness*, or *negligence* is inferior to *idleness*. The word *fuirre* is the comparative degree of *fa* or *fo* (under), and corresponds with the Latin word *lower* <note>original Latin: *inferius*</note> from *below* <note>original Latin: *infra*</note>, and means here less extensive in its signification and import, or applicable to fewer principles and cases. The kind of argument above mentioned is also used thus: every *idleness* is *negligence*, but every *negligence* is not *idleness*; for a man performs many actions through *negligence* which are not *idleness*, therefore *negligence* is more extensive than *idleness*.

**Desire over Utility**, *Desire* <term>Toiscide: will, desire, or inclination</term> is more extensive than *Utility* <term>Torba: profit, benefit, or use</term>, and the same kind of reasoning is made use of. N. B. Desire is the will, inclination, or intent. *Not every desire is utility, but every utility is desire; for a man may do many things through his desire that result in no utility, but he will do nothing through utility that is not also from desire; desire is therefore broader than utility.* <note>original Irish: "Ni can toiscide is torba, & gach torba is toiscide; uair do genead duine moran tria toiscide na bi torba, agus ni digne tria torba na bi toiscide; is forleite toiscide desin ina torba."</note>

---

(b) This reasoning is a true philosophical argument of an antecedent and consequent, called in philosophy an *Enthymeme* <term>Enthymema: a syllogism or logical argument where one premise is implied rather than stated</term> the antecedent whereof is clearly demonstrated. This distinction between the terms, together with the many others equally discernible in this fragment, proves that the Irish legislator was accurately versed in the philosophy of the great Aristotle, according to whom such terms are styled *more and less common terms.* <note>original Latin: "termini magis et minus communes"</note>

### ->BREHON LAWS.<-

**THE ASSISTANT IN THE HARM** <term>Frithaig Torba: literally 'counter-profit' or 'opposition of harm'; refers to an accessory or assistant in a crime</term>. **That is: the man who approaches the place where a harmful act is being committed** <note>original Irish: *fir indsaige do in baile imbith ag denam gnim torb*</note>; this means one who assists a second person to commit a crime against the law, and such a person is to make satisfaction in the one-third of the damage and penalty; **his satisfaction reaches to a third** <note>original Irish: *a dilfe do conuice a trian*</note>; the same is to hold good if having it in his power to hinder the principal aggressor's escape when condemned or judgment passed, and he does not hinder it; some think it only means a person who without any assistant infringes the law. <margin>An **assistant** <note>original Irish: *fritaig*</note> signifies any single man who commits a crime against the law, having another person to countenance and support him therein</margin>; to which it is added, that if the first person be acquitted and obliged to pay no <term>eric: a blood-fine or ransom paid in restitution for a crime, similar to the Anglo-Saxon 'wergild'</term> or pecuniary fine, the **approaching man** <term>fir indsaige: the person who enters the scene of the crime</term> or assistant will thereby be also exempted from paying one-third.

**THREE DIVISIONS OF TRESPASS.** <term>Teora fodla fogla: the threefold classification of legal injuries or damages</term>. **Trespass** <term>Fogla: damage, injury, or legal trespass</term> is three-fold, namely: by *species, genus, and subaltern* <note>original Latin/Irish: *gne cinel cinelach & subalter*</note>; by *cinel* is meant species, and by *gne* genus <note>The text notes that in this legal context, these philosophical terms are used to categorize the severity and type of crimes</note>; there is a *genus* in **intent** <term>comraite: deliberate intent or malice</term>, in **negligence** <term>tafot: also 'anfot', accidental injury or lack of care</term>, in **harm** <term>torba: a general term for a damaging act</term>, and in **defect** <term>teasba: a lack or omission</term>, but they are all species deriving under **wounding** <term>etguin: the overarching legal category for killing or physical injury</term>, which is more extensive than any of them, and the genus of all: **looking toward the wounding** <note>original Irish: *ag aitfeagad don etguin*</note>; nevertheless it derives itself under the four **trespasses** <note>original Irish: *fogla*</note>, and these again derive under the twelve divisions of **wounding** <note>original Irish: *etguin*</note>, so that the genus is under its genus as a subordinate category.

**JUDGE** <note>original Irish: *Bra*</note>, means the judge or <term>breheaman: a Brehon, the professional jurist and arbitrator in ancient Ireland</term>.

**WHERE IS THE CRIMINAL?** <note>original Irish: *Cid dechruithe*</note>, is a term signifying, where is the criminal?

**CORPSE** <note>original Irish: *Messa*</note>, means the dead body of a person; the **fine** <term>ic: a payment of restitution or compensation</term> must be paid when it is found without

The same holds good if the body be covered over or hidden, though found in a frequented place; it also takes place until such time as the guilty person owns the crime, or until it be proved against him according to law, or until legal compensation be made by him. And if the body hath perished in a trench or in water, provided it be not a hidden dyke, and in a lonesome place, there is no other <term>eric: a fine or restitution paid to the family of a victim</term> or fine to be paid but that of bare manslaughter.

(c) **Cumalas** <note>written as *Xma* in the original manuscript</note> is an abbreviation of *cumala*, and a <term>cumal</term> is three cows. Has not this law a great affinity with the **Levitical law** <note>The laws of the ancient Israelites found in the Old Testament</note>, where it is commanded that if any person was found slain, and it was not known who had slain him, **that an heifer, which had not been wrought with, should be substituted in the place of the murderer; and the judges shall measure unto the cities round about, and that city next to the slain shall find the heifer.** <note>See the Bible, Deuteronomy 21:1-3</note>. So our Irish law says, the <term>cric: a territory or boundary</term> is <term>nesa: nearest</term>, nearest or next.

• . • The Editor is happy to inform his correspondent C. that in the manuscripts of Trinity College, Dublin (class E table 3, No. 5) he has found the remaining part of the <term>blai: specific sections or exemptions within the law</term>; the book begins thus, *The place of this book is Aicill near Tara, and its time is the time of Cairbre Lifechar son of Cormac, and its author is Cormac* <note>original Irish: *Loc don liabar fo Aicill iraice Teamur, 7 aimsir do aimsir Coirpri Liffeachair mac Cormat 7. pearsa do Cormuc*</note>, i. e. The place where this book was written was Aicill near Tara, in the time of Cairbre Lifechar son of Cormac, and the person was Cormac. Then follows the history of Ceallach son of Cormac thus: Ceallach son of Cormac having by force taken away the daughter of Sorar son of Artcarb, Aongus Gabhuaidach <note>Aongus of the Terrible Spear, a famous legendary warrior</note> who was an <term>Arigh-eachta: a noble responsible for avenging insults to the community</term>, and at that time in the territory of Luighne, revenging some injustice done to his tribe, by chance went into the house of a woman of that territory, and forcibly took from her some milk and curds. "It would be more becoming you," says the woman, "to take revenge on Ceallach son of Cormac, in your niece's cause, than to rob me of my provisions." Disdaining to resent this language on the woman, he made directly towards Tara, where he arrived after sun-set. Now there was a law prohibiting any person coming armed into Tara after sun-set, so he went in unarmed, and taking down Cormac's spear from the place where it hung in the hall of Tara, he killed Ceallach son of Cormac on the spot, and drawing back the spear with great force, the ferrule <note>the metal cap at the end of the spear shaft</note> at the end struck out Cormac's eye and wounded the <term>reactaire: a royal steward or judge</term> of Tara in the back, of which he died. Then Cormac was sent to Aicill hard by Tara, to be cured; upon which the sovereignty of Ireland was conferred on Cairbre Lifechar, the son of Cormac, according to the established law, that no king having a corporeal blemish can reign in Ireland <note>In ancient Irish tradition, a king had to be physically "perfect" to maintain the prosperity of the land; Cormac's blindness disqualified him</note>. It is then added, that Tara was so situated it could be seen from Aicill, but Aicill could not be seen from Tara; and then begins the <term>Eitge: the "Book of Aicill," a major collection of criminal law</term>, one folio of which is wanting. The reader must notice that in this preface Aicill is described as the name of a place; in the fragment above-mentioned Aicill is said to be a Jurisconsult <note>a legal expert or judge</note>; this might proceed from the place being so called after the person.

<meta>catchword: judgment</meta>

Here ends the Fragment, so that all the rest of the <term>blai: specific sections of the law dealing with exemptions or legal protections</term> are wanting, and all that part composed by Cinfaela <note>Cenn Fáelad mac Aillila, a 7th-century Irish scholar-warrior famous for his prodigious memory</note> and promulgated by Donal mac Aeda mac Aimirech <note>Domnall mac Áedo, a High King of Ireland and King of Ulster in the 7th century</note> king of Ulster.

The impartial reader, after perusing this extract, will easily see that my distinction between the text of the law, and the explication of the text, is well grounded; that the former is of a much higher antiquity than the Christian era, though the latter was really the composition of a Christian. The style of the text is extremely ancient, and the language admitted of many expositions, even from a writer whose time seems to approach within a century or two at the most of St. Patrick’s. He sometimes gives many and opposite senses to the same term, and at other times cites the authority of the ancients in order to explain himself. In a word, every character discernible in the text, demonstrates its antiquity, and proves it is truly and genuinely the work of the Pagan <term>Juris-consult: an expert in law who provides legal opinions</term> and of the Pagan Irish princes to whom it is attributed; which being duly proved, most positively evinces the use of letters at least long before the epoch of Christianity in this island.

I do not recollect to have met any where with the words *cailc luibar* <note>original Irish: *cailc leabhair*</note>, a chalk book; it seems to denote that Cormac <note>Cormac mac Airt, a legendary High King of Ireland</note> used tablets, covered with some certain kind of matter, fit to smooth the surfaces, and to render them fit to inscribe, what he thought proper on them. The word *cailc* indeed means *chalk*, but it may also denote parchment rendered white by chalk. The true sense requires to be cleared up by other passages in our Irish monuments, and for this reason I leave it to the better <note>The text cuts off here, likely intended to read "to the better informed" or "to the better judgment of the reader."</note>

### BREHON LAWS.

judgment and literary researches of our antiquarians. Now as the laws regarding manslaughter form the first part of the section called *<term>na blai: specific sections of the law dealing with exemptions, liabilities, or legal protections</term>*, and that the explanation of the terms served only for a preparation to that part, I shall take notice of some curious and useful circumstances tending to illustrate those laws. The *<term>Eric: a body-price or fine paid in compensation for a killing or injury</term>* or fine for manslaughter specified in the above fragment, did not regard the person who committed the fact, but was payable to the provincial king, from the *<term>petty state: the 'tuath', or the basic political and territorial unit in ancient Ireland</term>* or principality wherein the body of a man or woman was found dead, unless the chief of that state had the particular privilege and faculty of having his people exempted from such a fine; which indeed was one of the most considerable immunities a provincial king could bestow on a chief of his province. 

There was a difference observed in the infliction of this fine, for if the fact was proved to be willful murder, the fine was rigorous, if involuntary, it was less rigorous, and in both cases proportioned to the dignity or status of the deceased person’s condition <note>In Brehon Law, the 'honor-price' of a person determined the value of the fine; a nobleman's death required a higher payment than that of a commoner</note>. And although the aggressor did suffer the penalty which the law inflicted, the district or state wherein the crime was perpetrated, was not thereby exempted in the least degree from paying the fine. This rigor obliged the natives of any district to be careful and vigilant in preserving each others lives, as well as of strangers, lest they should suffer through the malice and wickedness of others. And certain it is, that many misfortunes which we see happen daily amongst us, may be efficaciously prevented by such means; the natives of a district having it often in their power to prevent them. We must observe that the Irish fines are mostly specified by paying *cumalas*, which word—according to its modern acceptance since the Christian era—means only three cows; but I have somewhere met with that expression regarding Pagan times, wherein it denoted men or women slaves, guilty of crimes deserving death, who were delivered up as a part of the *Eric*, to be sacrificed to the <term>manes: the spirits of the deceased, a term borrowed from Roman mythology by the author to describe ancient Irish burial customs</term> of the deceased.

In the Essay on the *Antiquity of the Irish Language*, addressed to the Literati of Europe, the author gives us a specimen of a code of laws composed by *Sean son of Aigid* <note>Likely Sencha mac Ailella (or mac Aigid), a legendary jurist of ancient Ireland</note>, in the time of Fergus mac Leid, king of Ulster twenty-six years before the birth of Christ; it is called *<term>Seanchas mor: "The Great Tradition" or "Great Antiquity," the primary collection of ancient Irish Brehon Laws</term>*. Land that is given across the neck of the family, where the twelve tongues are stronger to overturn it than the one tongue that defends it.* <note>original: "Tir do beir i coibechi mna, nad bi maith, nad uidnaidet a folta coire. Tir do beir dar braigit fine, aratreiffu in da tengaid dec diathintud. Oldas in toen tenga doafend." This reflects the legal resistance to alienating family land through female marriage portions</note> And then follows, *Every semi-free tenant with his proper property does not pay for the crime of his son;* <note>original: "Gach fuidir conatothcus techta niica cinaid a meic." A <term>fuidir</term> was a tenant of lower social status who nonetheless had specific legal protections and responsibilities</note> which words are the conclusion of the foregoing part of the statute. The first words are an axiom of the law, equivalent to the Latin axiom regarding property, namely, *the thing cries out for its master.* <note>original: "res clamat Domino suo"</note> The Irish literally means, *every profit claims a return to the right owner, and the rest serve for a conclusion, namely, a son or male kindred, you shall pay no fine for the fault, meaning, that as right and justice favour you, the law shall not punish but protect you.*

The following words are the text of a new law on the same subject, namely, *of a grandson, of a great-grandson, of a great-great-grandson* <note>original: "nachai nachaiarmui nach aindui nach." These terms denote the specific degrees of kinship—nia, iarmua, and indua—required to determine inheritance and liability within a family group</note>; and the ensuing part to the end is an explication of this very old text which may be thus interpreted: *Of the proximity of blood, that person is to suffer the legal penalties to whose estate the fattened cattle belong; for the property of the furniture does not belong to him, except only in the case of necessity,* <note>original: "etgin." In Irish law, <term>éigin</term> can refer to necessity, compulsion, or specific instances of violence or distress that alter normal legal liability</note> *nor can he enjoy the rights of the son's offspring when the male line is not extinct; but the said person may possess the sheep which are fattened on the estate; if he goes beyond this, he must pay a fine for his fault, and bear the burden of his trespass.* The words run thus:

## B R E H O N  L A W S.

<meta>catchword: ments</meta>

his nearest of kin or for his own crime, the lord who sustains him <note>original: "idm-biatha" — referring to a lord who provides food or maintenance to a tenant</note> is he who pays for his crime. For he does not have the right to the honor-price <note>original: "dire" — a legal payment or fine based on an individual's social status</note> of his property, but only the value of its restitution; nor does he receive the honor-price of his son, nor any inheritance, nor fine, nor his mother; the lord who sustains him, it is he who takes these things, and pays for his crime, and supports his liabilities. <note>original: "a comoceus fine nach a cinaid fadeisin flathair idm-biatha ise iccass a cinaid. By the above specimen, the heads whereof appear to be compiled by a pagan author, and ordained or digested by the person to whom it is attributed, the truth of the existence of letters in Ireland before Christianity, is probably demonstrated. But this is not the only advantage the author seems desirous of pointing to, by this selection of that code, for it also gives us a clear and positive law, according to which all landed properties were to be vested in the male line, with an express exclusion of females even in the case of dowry or portion, and unless the male offspring of the family were extinct. This law was universal amongst the old Irish in primitive times, from the sceptre to the ploughshare, from the king to the lowest condition of men, who possessed landed property; it was universally received as a national maxim, flowing from the particular genius and spirit that characterised the natives, and from the political circumstances in which they found themselves situated, relatively to their neighbours, as well as from their own common views and public interests.

Thus that national and state maxim originally established among the Franks, after their first settlements

# FRAGMENT OF THE

settlements in Gaul, by which all female heirs were excluded from any share in the inheritances, which they called Salic lands, <note>Salic lands refers to the *Terra Salica*, land held by the Salian Franks which, under Salic Law, could not be inherited by women.</note> was not only the result of their military genius, but also a necessary measure of public expediency, for extending their conquests on the ruins of the Roman empire. Yet it should not be judged that the Franks were either the first, or the only nation that adopted it; no, for it was certainly common to other nations that carefully observed it, with regard to all landed tenures of a like original constitution with the Salic lands.

We have good authority to say, that a great part of them were the same individual lands that had been set apart by the Roman emperors, particularly by Alexander Severus, <note>Roman Emperor from 222 to 235 AD, known for his administrative reforms.</note> in the year of Christ 222, as pensionary livings, called *military benefices*, <note>original: *beneficia militaria* — grants of land given to soldiers in exchange for military service, a precursor to the feudal system.</note> and appropriated to those officers who were charged with the defence of certain provinces. Now as the right of the proprietors was conditional, and depended on personal military service, *females* were naturally excluded from any share in them. The Franks being possessed of these according as they fell vacant, under their king Clovis <note>Clovis I (c. 466–511), the first King of the Franks to unite all the Frankish tribes under one ruler.</note> and his sons, the Salic laws made no change in their tenure, but left them still subject to the obligation of military service. Hence that express clause of excluding women and their race, from sharing in those state or Salic lands, so fundamental in the French constitution, took its first rise. And in effect since the very Crown and its royal landed properties, were the first and principal Salic fief, a female succession with regard to the Crown, must be highly inconsistent

It connects these land-use customs to the foundational Frankish laws under King Clovis and the eventual French constitutional ban on female succession to the throne.</summary>

# BREHON LAWS

inconsistent with the fundamental maxims of the French state.

According to the tenure of our Irish laws, all females were excluded from having any share in the chief administration of affairs, as well with regard to the government of the whole kingdom, as to its <term>petty states</term> <note>The "petty states" refer to the *túatha*, the numerous small, autonomous kingdoms that existed across ancient Ireland.</note> and inferior principalities; for no other reason, without doubt, than that these states required military service, for which the female sex was disqualified by nature. In effect they found themselves situated among different tribes of people, whose principal representatives were sometimes ambitious, and unjust enough to encroach on their landed properties; the false glory of being esteemed more powerful and more expert in war than their neighbours, joined to their interests and the advancement of their respective trials, <note>The transcription reads "trials," which in this context likely refers to the legal claims or historical "tribes" of the different families.</note> often determined these men to disturb the public quiet, and endeavour to raise themselves on the ruins of others. 

Hence followed a political necessity in every branch or <term>sept</term>, <note>A "sept" is a traditional Gaelic clan division, consisting of a group of families who share a common surname and inhabit a specific territory.</note> to choose out for their chief and leaders, men of known valour and experience, to fight their battles successively, and to defend their landed properties against all invaders. And hence the maxims of excluding women from inheriting landed properties was as sacred amongst the ancient Irish, as amongst the Franks, or any other people. And as the lands of all the petty states did absolutely and indispensably exclude women from inheriting them, so the same law gradually became general, and descended to the lowest denomination of land proprietors.

# FRAGMENT OF THE [BREHON LAWS]

The above Irish specimen describes the injustice of alienating the lands from the male line, in favor of women, by the expressive term of **LAND TAKEN OVER THE FAMILY’S NECK.** <note>original: "TIR DO BEIR DAR BRAIGIT FINE." This striking idiom suggests that giving land to a female heir was seen as an act of violence or a forced bypass of the natural male succession, as if something were being pushed across one's throat.</note> At this day when a man encroaches on any lands, and takes it to farm, by underhand dealing and unknown terms, *he took it over my neck*, <note>original: "do ghlac ſe tar mo braigid è"</note> or else, as *it was a treacherous act he performed to take my land out over my neck*; <note>original: "gniomh fill do rin ſe mo thalamb do ghlaca thar mo bhraghaid amach"</note> by which it would appear, that when the male line of one family was extinct, the estate was to devolve on the <term>sept</term> <note>A clan division or group of families sharing a common ancestor.</note> or <term>fine</term>, <note>The Irish word for a kinship group or family unit.</note> and became the property of their males.

The same law was observed by other European nations; the Goths and Vandals excluded women from succeeding to the throne. We have a very remarkable instance of this, in the history of *Amalasuntha*, daughter to the famous *Theodoric*, (and mother of *Athelric*) king of the *Ostrogoths*. This princess, whose bright genius and eminent knowledge of the Greek and Latin tongues will always do her memory honor, was nevertheless disqualified to reign after the death of her son, on account of her sex, and married *Theodatus*, with a view to exercise all regal power under his name, in which however she found herself fatally mistaken in 534. See *Cassiodorus, Variae, book 10.* <note>original: "Caſſiodor. var. lib. 10." Cassiodorus was a Roman statesman and writer who served under Theodoric the Great.</note>

The Roman empire and the Imperial Crown excluded women from ever inheriting it. Thus when Attila king of the Huns, surnamed the scourge of God, demanded in marriage Honoria sister to Valentinian III. with a view of sharing in

# BREHON LAWS.

...in the empire, by virtue of her right, the emperor answered, that even in case he were married to Honoria, he could derive no right from her, inasmuch as women had none to the empire. **Neither was the empire owed to Honoria; for the Roman empire belonged to men, not women.** <note>original: "Neque imperium Honoriæ debéri ; virorum enim non mulierum Romanum imperium esse."</note> See Priscus Rhetor. <note>A 5th-century Greek historian and diplomat.</note> Duchesne, volume I. page 223. <note>Referring to André Duchesne, a prominent 17th-century French historian often called the father of French history.</note>

The Irish in primitive times were not only attentive in observing strictly the above law, in favour of the male line, from a political and necessary principle, but they also observed distributive justice <note>A philosophical concept of fairness in how rewards and costs are shared within a group.</note>, in sharing equitably with the males of their respective families. They shewed particular honour to their chiefs, and these in return did not despise their inferiors, in the distribution of goods. The heads of families considered that the support of their friends and relations in time of war, was absolutely necessary, both for repelling the power of their enemies and advancing their military conquests and therefore they judged that the sanction of rewards and a fair and honest partition of landed properties, which were often the fruit of their common valour, was the most firm and efficacious cement of union, that could subsist between the prince and his relations. Hence the very nature of these military tenures <note>Land held by a person on condition of providing military service to a lord or chief.</note> rendered them capable of being divided and subdivided between the males of the first proprietors, and hence the law of gavelling <note>From "Gavelkind," a system of inheritance where land is divided equally among the sons or male heirs of a deceased person, rather than passing solely to the eldest son.</note> them took its rise, and was as universally observed in this kingdom, as the very law of excluding the female sex from any share in them, has been proved to be...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Irish land inheritance, moving from the exclusion of women mentioned on page 11 to the specific system of "gavelling" or Gavelkind, which distributed land among male family members. -># GAVEL LAW<-

This gavelling settlement <note>Gavelkind was a system of inheritance where land was divided equally among all sons, rather than going only to the eldest (primogeniture).</note> the Irish called GABHALTUS CINE, or GABHAIL CINE, literally meaning FAMILY-SETTLEMENT; from which the English formed the term GAVEL-KIND. GABHALTUS or GABHAIL, pronounced *Gavail*, in Irish signifies any landed settlement, lately acquired by conquest, by inheritance, or by contract; but its etymological meaning is CONQUEST. Thus by the words LEABHAR NA GABHALA is understood the Book of Conquests; <note>original: "LEABHAR NA GABHALA", a foundational medieval Irish text detailing the legendary settled waves of inhabitants in Ireland.</note> and in the Irish history of Thomond <term>Thomond: A historical kingdom in North Munster, primarily modern County Clare.</term> called *The Triumphs of Turlough*, <note>original: "Caithreim Toirdealbhaig", a 14th-century account of the wars between the O’Brien dynasties.</note> describing the bloody wars carried on between the O'Briens of Thomond and those of Arra, <term>Arra: A barony in modern County Tipperary where a branch of the O'Briens settled.</term> in the thirteenth century, the settlement or estate which the latter possessed themselves of in the country of Arra, is called GABHALTUS.

The Franks observed the same law of dividing the tenures called Salic lands. <note>Lands governed by the Salic Law, which famously excluded female inheritance to ensure military service was provided by the landholder.</note> Abbé Dubos <note>Jean-Baptiste Dubos (1670–1742), a French historian and critic.</note> shows a remarkable instance of that truth; he quotes for his voucher the celebrated Bodin, <note>Jean Bodin (1530–1596), an influential French jurist and political philosopher.</note> citing the testamentary act of a gentleman of Guyenne, <note>A historical province in southwest France.</note> whereby the father divides between his sons, the Salic or feudal lands of which he was proprietor. And in the same place, this author observes, that in all appearance those military benefices <note>Grants of land given to soldiers in exchange for their service.</note> were the true origin of the landed properties called **NOBLE FIEFS**, <note>original: "FIEFS NOBLE"</note> or tenures in military service.

The Anglo-Saxons also observed this custom; it is well known they divided their landed interests between the possessor's children, which they called in their language **GIFE-AL-KIN**, <note>Likely a folk etymology for "Gavelkind," interpreted here as "give to all kin."</note> a word nearly similar in sound and in meaning to the Irish term GABHAIL-CINE.

# G A V E L  L A W,

It may now be objected that these examples of the existence of the law of **Gavel kind** <term>Gavel kind: An ancient Irish inheritance custom (Gavelkind) where land was divided among all sons or male members of a kin-group, rather than the English system of primogeniture where only the eldest son inherits.</term> only regards the Irish in the time of Christianity, and is no way pertinent to the design of this essay, which should principally tend to illustrate the laws, customs, manners and literature of the pagan Irish. My answer is, that the introduction and establishment of the Christian religion amongst us did not change, or meddle in the political or civil laws of the nation, that were just in themselves, and adapted to the genius of the people; it only rectified what was vicious or unjust in them, and squared them to the interior law of conscience, and the dictates of good reason, securely directed by the gospel. Hence the conversion of a people to Christianity did not affect their political principles of government, nor cause the least alteration in their civil statutes, when these were equitable. # G A V E L   L A W,

I am confident that the Irish will find it enacted amongst their laws, in the literary researches into antiquity; and hence I may conclude with a certain degree of plausibility, that although no proofs taken from authentic writings of pagan times could be now exhibited, nevertheless a simple perspective of the examples above cited will sufficiently evince its having existed in this kingdom long before St. Patrick.

Upon all these different specimens of Irish Gavels <term>gavel: derived from the Irish "gabhail," referring here to the system of Gavelkind where land is divided among heirs.</term>, several observations do naturally occur. First, that either the fathers or the elders of families generally maintained a fair and sometimes an equal distribution of shares between the males of said families, of which we see perfect examples in the preceding land-divisions. Secondly, that the *natural sons* <note>Sons born outside of a formal marriage.</note> received their equal shares just as the sons born in lawful marriage did. From both of these circumstances, we have reason to conclude that this custom was grounded on the patriarchal or primitive law of nature. Consequently, it was derived from a principle of a much more ancient establishment than the laws of Christian princes, by which *bastards* are excluded from inheriting the family estate while legitimate children are still living. Thus, according to this same primitive law, Jacob’s sons by the handmaids of his wives Leah and Rachel are ranked among the *heads of the twelve tribes of Israel*, on an equal level in

# GAVEL LAW,

point of landed property, with the sons of his lawful wives, and their descendants entitled to enjoy their shares of the land of promise <note>A biblical allusion comparing Irish ancestral lands to the Promised Land of the Hebrew patriarchs.</note>, as well as those sprung from the other children of that patriarch.

It is also remarkable, that it was generally the senior, and not the direct heir in lineal descent from the common stock, who was qualified to make this gavel <term>gavel: from the Irish "gabhail," referring to the system of Gavelkind where land is divided among male heirs.</term>, because he was the chief proprietor; of this we see a clear instance in the Cuanagh <note>Uí Cuanach, a territory in East Limerick.</note> gavel made by Conor the Great O'Brien, whose first cousin Turlogh son of Thady, surnamed the Fighter <note>original: *Anchomraic*</note> was the direct heir, this Thady being the elder brother of Mortogh, father to Conor the Great. Notwithstanding we see that Conor, by his right of seniority, was chief of the family, proprietor of its landed estates, and solely qualified to make the gavel; the text runs thus: *This Conor gave half of the portion of the land division that fell to himself to the two sons of Thady the Fighter, they being his senior kinsmen* <note>original: "Tug an Conchur morsa leath na codach ranna fearainn rainig e fein do dhibh mhac Taidg an chomhraic air a mbeith na sinfior braithrech aige"</note>; he explains this matter fully by the following words, *this Muirtheartach [Murtagh], father of Conor the Great, was younger than Thady the Fighter* <note>original: "dob oge an Muirtheartach so athair Chonchubhair mhoir na Taidhg an chomhraic"</note>.

Another observation, equally curious, may be noticed, viz. that though the chief or senior stints himself to a bare equality of share with every other male of the family, yet he reserves the chief property of the estate as vested in himself during his life, by subjecting all the other shares to a chief rent, which was to be paid him in an humble and dutiful manner. This was doubtless slight and inconsiderable, nevertheless it was a sufficient mark both of his authority over them, and of their dependency on him, as their chief lord; in a word,

<summary>This page explains that under Irish Gavel Law, the senior member of a family—rather than the direct heir—held the authority to divide the land, maintaining lordship through the collection of a symbolic 'chief rent.'</summary>

->EXPLAINED.<-

the equality of shares, showed a real community of goods between the members of the family, and the reserved chief rent <note>A payment made to a superior lord or the head of a clan to acknowledge his ownership of the land.</note>, both secured the respect due to the senior, and declared the property of the estate to be vested in him alone. Hence what Strabo <note>A Greek geographer and historian from the 1st century AD.</note> applies to the *Asiatic Iberians* <note>Ancient inhabitants of the Caucasus; historical writers often drew parallels between them and the Irish (Hibernians).</note> was equally applicable to the *Irish nation* in former times. "These people hold all things in common within families; but he who is the senior rules and holds the property." <note>original: *Hi omnia per familias communia habent; sed is imperat et rem habet qui senior est.*</note>

Now as the reserve of chief rent on the gavelled <term>gavelled: land divided under the system of Gavelkind, where property was partitioned among male heirs rather than going solely to the eldest son.</term> lands, not only established the sway, and influence of the chief lord, or head of the family, over all the members of the tribe, and likewise secured for him a reversion <note>The legal right for land to return to the original owner or their heirs under certain conditions.</note> of the estate when the actual tenants either forfeited or died without issue; so this custom was not, and could not be then attended with any consequences so destructive of the splendor of a family, as it must be in our days. In ancient times the dignity of a chief did not depend on pecuniary <note>Financial or monetary.</note> revenues; it consisted in his power and influence over the tribe he governed, in the affluence of provisions he had for his household, and in the number of fighting men he could command to vindicate his rights, to assist his friends, or to enlarge his conquests. And as to the splendor of the tribe in general, it consisted in their numbers, in the full and peaceable enjoyment of their rights, and in their capacity of furnishing their family chiefs with their different supplies.

In fine, the Gavel custom, as it was observed by the ancient Irish, appears to have been perfectly reconcileable with the exigencies of the state, and with the dignity of any chieftain, were he even a

### GAVEL LAW,

sovereign prince. And why not? since it was grounded on the law of nature and reason, the law of distributive justice and equity; and since it was adapted to times and circumstances of political government, in which it could not be productive of any consequences prejudicial to public or private economy; it secured their prerogatives to the eldest, and their birthrights to the younger brothers of a family, far from qualifying the latter, in any casual circumstances, to usurp the natural rights of the former.

A character of so different an organ, as is the palate from the lips, would make us incline to think, that the letter P not being used at all by the pagan Irish, the missionaries adopted C as a character most frequently used amongst them, and consequently the first preachers of Christianity in Ireland, found their pagan disciples in possession of this letter C, as well as of the other letters necessary for expressing their language articulately.

Thus in all words begun or ended by X, instead of writing that simple character, they never chose to represent it, otherwise than by employing two of the Roman characters, namely *gs* or *cs*, a trouble they certainly might have saved themselves, at least in writing the Latin, had they not rejected it as an exotic character, and not existing in their *ancient alphabet*; if this was not the true motive, I candidly acknowledge, the case seems to me a paradox <note>A seemingly absurd or self-contradictory statement that may nonetheless be true</note>; for if the Irish had no letters before the introduction of the Latin alphabet, what could be their motive constantly to reject some simple characters, and substitute two different letters in their stead, especially in writing a foreign tongue, to which all such characters were equally proper and fitting; and if *all* letters were equally new and exotic to them, certainly *all* had an equal right to be preserved by them? Yet did they admit X as a numeral <note>While they avoided 'X' for phonetic spelling (as in 'ks'), they accepted it as the Roman symbol for the number ten</note>.

This is not the only instance I find of the same economy <note>A linguistic "economy" refers to the specific system or set of rules governing how a language is written or spoken</note> being practised by the Irish writers of ancient times, for when occasion offered of writing **v consonant** <note>The letter 'V' as a consonant sound, which in early Irish was often represented by other letters like 'f', 'u', or 'b'</note> so natural to the Latin, they always

Now as this conduct of the first Irish converts, with regard to certain letters of the Latin alphabet, seems directly opposite to all good economy, whereas by receiving a few more of them, especially the *v* consonant, would have spared them expence of time and labour and vellum, objects not unworthy their care, so their constancy in adhering to so injudicious a practice, affords almost a proof and argument in favour of the prejudices of their former education and letters, whereof they intended

to leave us clear vestiges, even on its very ruins (g). This quality of preserving old customs and usages is so innate from of old in Irishmen that no nation can claim it with more justice, innovation being at all times diametrically opposite to their character <note>original: "genius"</note>.

If the old Irish had no letters, no alphabet of their own fashion, with a peculiar manner of employing them for expressing the organic sounds of their language, and for preserving the original structure of their words—an art wherein all true <term>orthography</term> <note>the conventional spelling system of a language</note> consists—it is apparent from the very nature of things that they could never have thought of using the labial letter **M** with the aspirate **H** subjoined to it, to render the sound of the Latin *v* consonant.

(g) The learned antiquarian Thomas Hearne has selected a Roman inscription, which clearly proves that we know not what power the ancient Romans gave to several letters.

Just as among the Romans the letter B very often had the same value as V, as is very well known to those who study ancient monuments; this is certainly seen in this inscription found in Fabrettus <note>Raffaele Fabretti (1618–1700), an Italian scholar who specialized in ancient inscriptions</note>:

*bibenti* <note>drinking</note> signifies nothing other than *viventi* <note>living</note>, although the correction immediately following, *VIXIT* <note>lived</note>, seems to leave it in doubt whether it should be understood as referring to a drunkard, or to one leading a sober and frugal life. ### LITERATURE OF THE IRISH

reform, in a way that would lose the root words and traditional spelling <term>orthography</term> <note>the conventional system of spelling a language</note>. During the reign of Queen Elizabeth I, or at the latest by the time of King Henry VIII, the Irish language—like all other European languages—began to take on a new form; but as soon as the natives attempted this, the English reformers took steps to wipe out its traces in Ireland. This has been the primary reason for the language retaining its pure Celtic nature and spelling; the Irish people at that time, being determined to preserve it in opposition to these efforts, had no time to continue that process of refinement, which would likely have disguised its basic Celtic structure, making it similar to what we see in the Welsh dialect today.

This strategy by the English reformers, far from being sensible or effective in achieving their intended purpose and goals, was in fact the exact opposite of both. For to persuade any people to adopt a new opinion or a new form of worship, it defies all logic to think that the method for achieving it should be to exhort them in a foreign language; for in that case, the reformers must first have had the trouble of teaching them this new language, or else wait until the people first rejected their own dialect. This native dialect was just as elegant and as fitting as the language used by the reforming ministers of that time to express every thought of the human heart, and to convince listeners of any truth whatsoever, whether in literary or religious matters. And this short-sightedness <note>original: "blindness"</note> of the reformers regarding their practice of always praying and preaching in English has been attributed to a special act of divine providence <note>the protective care of God</note> in favor of the Roman Catholic religion, and to the holy prayers

Now, as to the absolute orders and command which, it is said, had been imposed on the primitive Irish converts by their spiritual guides and superiors to lay aside the use of their ancient pagan characters; I am far from thinking it was altogether founded on the notion of some preachers of the gospel (mentioned by the author of the *Irish Historical Library* following Verelius), by which they represented those characters as if they had really been the handwriting of the Devil. It is evident, the author of the <term>Book of Lecan</term> <note>A major medieval Irish manuscript containing historical, genealogical, and hagiographical material, compiled around 1400</note>, who lived long after the epoch of Christianity, had no sort of view of doing honour to this colony of people, but was rather inclined to depreciate their real merit, so his ascribing to them excellent verses before the Christian era in Ireland, is a pretty strong proof, that the use of letters was well known before that time; for the text says, *that until the coming of the faith, these <term>dans</term> were not banished by reason of their goodness;* it is impossible to conceive how these excellent verses could be preserved by the help of oral tradition only, to the time of St. Patrick’s arrival; or how could the new converts destroy or extirpate them, unless they had been committed to writing.
