The reader must understand, that these antiquarians only transcribed their voluminous pieces of history and genealogy out of other writings, without the least discernment or criticism, and in the dress wherein they found them recorded, insomuch that the style used in relating facts near their own times, is as different from that made use of for recording the deeds of remote antiquity, as our modern English differs from that spoken in the days of Henry IV. and V. in England.

This defect of critical examination in them, produces a double advantage to us, 1st, we have a view of the manners of the ancient Irish, 2d, we are fully instructed in the style and language. By the first, we are enabled to pass a right judgment on those ancient times; and thus when we find notions seriously delivered for truths, that are notoriously incompatible with the first principles of Christianity, and are known to be the tenets of pagan persuasion, then no reasonable cause of doubt remains for us to conclude, that such writings are of pagan times.

This truth can be exemplified in a most striking instance, from the <term>Book of Lecan</term>, wherein the pagan system of the <term>transmigration of souls</term> <note>original: "metempsychosis," the philosophical belief that the soul moves into a new body after death</note> is gravely exhibited. Thus *Pythagoras* <note>A famous Greek philosopher (c. 570–495 BC) known for his mathematical theorem and his belief in the reincarnation of souls</note> pretended he knew and remembered in what bodies his soul resided, before he was styled Pythagoras; first, he was *Cethalidus* <note>Likely Aethalides, a son of Mercury who was granted the gift of never-fading memory</note>, the supposed son of *Mercury*; next, he became *Euphorbus*, who was slain by *Menelaus* at the siege of *Troy*; afterwards, he was *Hermotimus*; then he became a fisherman of *Delos* by name *Pyrrhus*; and at last he became *Pythagoras*; and he also affirmed, that he well remembered all these different transmigrations; that he suffered in Hell, and saw others suffer likewise. Let it be remembered, that this man, by the clear light of reason, discovered that useful demonstration, of the square of the hypothenuse being equal to the sum of the two squares, &c. &c. <note>The Pythagorean theorem (a² + b² = c²), a fundamental principle in geometry</note> which proved as useful in mathematical solutions, as it was agreeable to this great philosopher.

->AN ENQUIRY, etc.<-

**BEN-ANAK**, the children or tribe of **ANAK**, the Anakites being the leading tribe of the whole group, similar to the Irish tribes Mac-Mahon, Mac-Carthy, etc. Although it must be admitted that the urgency of their circumstances had first forced the *Phoenicians* to engage in naval voyages, they nevertheless gained great benefits from that necessity. They surpassed all nations of the world in sailing and trade <note>original: "traffick"</note>, and made useful discoveries of islands in the European seas, the Mediterranean, in Egypt, Greece, Spain, etc., which were previously uninhabited.

Ben-Anak literally means the sons of giants or heroes; which is certainly the meaning the Irish gave the word **FENE** and **FEINE**; thus, to this day **FEINEAG** or **FEINIG** is used in old records and songs to mean a champion, a hero, or a giant. The author of a learned work on the basic elements of languages criticizes the great Bochart <note>Samuel Bochart (1599–1667), a renowned French scholar of biblical geography who argued that many European place names had Phoenician roots</note>, for not having understood the word *Phoenicians* to be of the same meaning as the Chaldean <note>An ancient Semitic language from Mesopotamia, often associated with the biblical patriarchs</note> word *Canaanite*. For just as *Canaanite* meant a merchant or negotiator in that language—from the Chaldean root <note>original Latin: "radix"</note> *Canaan*, a merchant—so does *Phoenician* mean the same thing in Greek, says this author. For *PEN* and *PHEN* mean money, trade, or usury <note>The practice of lending money at interest</note>; thus **PHENINIM** <note>original Hebrew: פְּנִינִים (peninim), meaning pearls or precious stones</note> also signifies riches or jewels, and **PÆNUS** <note>likely a variant of the Latin "faenus," meaning interest or usury</note> in Latin is usury. He opposes the same learned author’s opinion, that the Phoenicians abandoned their old name of Canaanites, on account of the infamy they were subject to, through the curse pronounced against their progenitor Canaan <note>Referring to the "Curse of Ham" in Genesis 9, where Canaan was cursed to be a "servant of servants"</note>; the reason he gives for this correction is as insufficient as the above, witness, says he, the Canaanite woman mentioned in Matthew 15 and 22, who came from the environs of Tyre and Sidon. This proves nothing; for the evangelists without doubt mentioned her extraction from Canaanites, as they had been accused by God, and particular orders given by him for their utter extirpation <note>Complete destruction or removal</note>, that so the office of our redeemer may be more conspicuous <note>Visible or obvious</note>, while he despised not the afflicted offspring of an accursed people; so that this is not the language of a Phoenician writer, but of a Christian evangelist. Some interpreters think, she was called Canaanite from a town situated in Phoenicia, bordering on the lands allotted to the tribe of Asher <note>original: "Aser"</note>, which was called Cana, whereof there is also mention made in Joshua, chapter 19, where it is expressly said, that the boundaries of the tribe of Asher were Abdon <note>original: "ABRAN"</note>, Rehob <note>original: "ROHOB"</note>, Hammon <note>original: "HAMON"</note> and Kanah <note>original: "CANA"</note>, unto the great Sidon. If this interpretation be true, his argument becomes void; Saint Mark calls this woman a **SYROPHOENICIAN**, either because the Syrians at different periods incorporated with the Phoenicians, or else to distinguish them from the Carthaginian, who were sometimes called **LIBYO-PHOENICIANS** <note>Phoenician settlers in North Africa, distinguished from the eastern Phoenicians of the Levant</note>.

### FIRST INHABITANTS OF IRELAND.
<meta>catchword: who</meta>

The word **ANAK** <note>Hebrew: "Anak," typically referring to a race of giants in the Bible</note>, which means a giant or hero, seems to be the root of the Greek *anax* (possessive form *anaktos*), the usual term for a king. The translators of the Septuagint <note>The ancient Greek version of the Old Testament</note> translate the Hebrew words **MELECH** and **MELCHI**, meaning a king, into *anax* or *anakta* <note>original: "anax, anakta, or anacta" (Greek variations of "lord" or "king")</note>. This, however, was not its original and proper meaning; it first signified a "savior" or "defender." Since this was the true duty of a sovereign—and the reason for creating a king was to defend and protect a people or family from destruction or oppression—it was natural that it should become the primary title for a king.

Homer calls Agamemnon and others by this name; Jupiter is styled the *anax* of gods and men. Nevertheless, the lesser gods are styled *anaktes* <note>original: "anaktes" (Greek: plural of anax)</note> to signify they are defenders or saviors. Asty-anax <note>Literally "Lord of the City"</note> was a title the Trojans gave to Scamandrius, the son of Hector, which according to Homer’s interpretation meant a "defender of the city." See *Iliad*, book vi, line 399. Now, the Irish word **ANAC** or **ANAIK** means to *save* or *defend*; thus we say *anaic sinn a Thiarna* <note>original: "anaic sinn a Thiarna" (Irish: Save/protect us, O Lord)</note>. The word *anacal* means protection, or the safeguard of a prince. Additionally, *anac clan* (sometimes written *eneaclan*) is the term used in the Brehon laws <note>The ancient native legal system of Ireland, which survived until the 17th century</note> for the tribute paid by the clan or tribe to the chief in exchange for his protection.

The obscure traditional accounts preserved in the old Irish manuscripts—and revived by Keating <note>Geoffrey Keating, a 17th-century historian who wrote the "History of Ireland" (Foras Feasa ar Éirinn)</note> and others—claim that the ancestors of the first inhabitants of Ireland first settled in Crete and other islands of the Mediterranean Sea, as well as in Egypt, Greece, Spain, and elsewhere. These accounts apparently point the reader toward the history and migrations of the *Phoenicians* <note>original: "Phenicians"</note> after they were expelled under the leadership of Cadmus <note>In Greek mythology, the Phoenician prince who founded Thebes and introduced the alphabet to the Greeks</note>,

<summary>The author links the Hebrew word for giant ("Anak") to Greek and Irish terms for "king" and "protector," arguing that leadership was originally defined by the duty to defend. He then connects Irish origin myths involving the Mediterranean to the historical migrations of the Phoenicians.</summary>

The most ancient Irish dialect is called **BEARLA NA FEINE** <note>Irish: "The Speech of the Fenians," a term used in medieval Irish literature for a specific poetic or legal language</note>, or **BESCNA NA FENE** <note>Irish: "The Customs/Language of the Fene," referring to the legendary early inhabitants of Ireland</note>, which means the dialect of the Fenians, the tongue of the Fenians. It is certain, the truth cannot be so well supported on the part of the Irish, they were too distantly situated from each other, and the time of their separation too remote to be observed but in an obscure manner; whereas the contiguous situation of Tyre and Carthage, made the renewal of their friendship no way difficult; in effect, we find a strict and inviolable union to have always

The Irish call the month of May *Bel-tine*, or fire of Belus <note>Belus is a Latinized form of the Semitic deity "Bel" or "Baal," associated with the sun and fertility</note>, and the first day of May *la Bel-tine*, or the day of Belus’s fire; they call the eve of the first of November *oidche Shamhna* <note>original: "oidche Shamhna", modern Irish: "Oíche Shamhna," the eve of Samhain or Halloween</note> (corruptly pronounced ee owna) or the eve of Samen, which was the Carthaginian name of the Sun. In those days, continues the author, malefactors were burnt between two fires; hence, when they would express a man to be in a great difficulty, they say he is *between two fires of Bel*, which in their language they express thus, *between two fires of Bel* <note>original: "edir dha thinne Bheal", modern Irish: "idir dhá thine Bheal"</note>, p. 105.

In some parts, as the counties of Waterford and Kilkenny, the brides, married since the last May-day, are compelled to furnish the young people with a ball covered with gold lace and another covered with silver lace, finely adorned with silver tassels; the price of these sometimes amounts to two guineas <note>A significant sum of money in the 18th century, equivalent to several weeks' wages for a laborer</note>; these balls, the symbols of the Sun and Moon, are suspended in a hoop ornamented with flowers, which hoop represents the circular path of Belus <note>A Latinized form of "Bel" or "Baal," a deity the author associates with the sun</note> or the Sun; and in this manner, they walk in procession from house to house. On the eve of St. John <note>June 23rd, the night before the feast of St. John the Baptist, traditionally celebrated with bonfires across Europe</note> another bonfire is lighted universally through the kingdom; on this night every family extinguishes the fire, which must be relighted from the bonfire; a lighted stick is also thrown with solemnity into the cabbage garden, to cause the roots to grow, and the young people run through one another with lighted sticks in their hands. This is not a pagan custom, but handed down from the first establishment of Christianity on the continent; for though the council of Elvira <note>A church synod held in Roman Spain around 305–306 AD, which issued several canons regarding Christian conduct and the suppression of pagan remnants</note> abolished the custom of most of the pagan fires, which had continued some centuries after Christianity; the illumination of the eve of St. John the Baptist still continued, the tradition of which is coeval <note>existing at the same time</note> with the prediction he made of Jesus Christ; which fire St. Bernard <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), an influential French abbot</note> notices to his fraternity, was become so universally practised in his time, that it was even observed among the Saracens <note>A medieval term for Muslims</note> and Turks. See *Homilies on the feast of John the Baptist* <note>original: "Homil. in fest Ioan. Bapt."</note>.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into Irish place names and linguistics that suggest an ancient connection to sun worship (the deity Belus). ### AN ENQUIRY INTO THE

Some Mountains in Ulster still bear the name of Beltane <note>Original: *Bel-tine*, the Gaelic festival marking the beginning of summer</note>; but in the southern part of the kingdom the name more frequently occurs. At the foot of Knockmealdown <note>Original: *Knocmaoldown*</note> mountain, near Clogheen in the county of Tipperary, is *Logh Bheal* or Belus’s Lake; on the Monavullagh <note>Original: *Moanmhullagh*</note> mountains, not far distant from this lake, is the Gap of Belus on the summit <note>Original Irish: *Barn na Bheal a mullach*</note>; the usual salutation of the common people was "The God Belus to you" <note>Original: *Bel de dhuit*. The author likely interprets *Bel* as the deity and *de* as *Dé* (God). It is more likely a variation of a blessing involving the word *Bail* (success or prosperity)</note>; the meaning of which not being understood by the present race, they now say "A mark from God to you" <note>Original: *Bal o Dhia dhuit*</note>, which they interpret thus, "a mark from God to you"; *bal* signifying a spot or blemish, a very improper term for a salutation; this is peculiar to the counties of Waterford and Kilkenny.

The month of May was indeed the most proper season of the year to acknowledge the beneficent favours of Belus or the Sun; as the month of November was, to acknowledge their gratitude to the same deity; because in May, that great planet begins to beautify the face of the earth; to nourish its decayed plants and vegetables, and to put life and warmth into its animal beings; and in November the harvest and the vintage is gathered into the barn. Hence of all created objects, that planet deserved most to be noticed and loved by rational sublunary <note>A term for things belonging to the earth, literally "under the moon"</note> beings, because its benign influence produced them health of body, and an acceptable prospect of nourishment. And hence it was, without doubt, that almost every pagan nation adored this beautiful planet as the parent of nature, under different names and appellations; a religion, which as Mr. Young observes in his *Revelation*, p. 35.

# FIRST INHABITANTS OF IRELAND.

<meta>The text continues from the previous page's mention of the harvest being gathered into the barn.</meta>

The worship of the sun took its rise in Chaldea <note>An ancient region in the southern part of Babylonia, modern-day Iraq</note>, was soon carried into Egypt, and from thence to Greece; it spread itself also to the most distant parts of the world, and infected not only the eastern but the western Scythians <note>Ancient nomadic people of Central Asia and Eastern Europe</note> and Tartars, but the Mexicans too, for the Spaniards found it there. (See Gage's *new Survey of the West Indies*, ch. 12.) Even the descendants of Shem, whose posterity preserved the memory of the true God for a longer time than those of Ham or Japhet <note>The three sons of Noah in biblical tradition</note>, at length transferred their homage to the Sun and Moon. (Photius from Ctesias. Quintus Curtius, book 8, chapter 9. Philostratus, book 3, chapter 35.)

The ancient practice of adoring the Sun by the symbol of fire, was first introduced into the world by ***Nimrod***, otherwise called ***Baal*** or ***Belus***, which in the Hebrew, Syriac and Phoenician, literally meant lord, or master. Belus is justly considered by the learned to be the first who withdrew a considerable number of people, employed by him in building Babylon, from the true worship of God to the spurious adoration of the Sun by fire. This idolatrous mode of worship soon overspread the earth, the Canaanites or Phoenicians observed it in the same manner with the pagan Irish. We read in the fourth book of Kings <note>In the Douay-Rheims Bible, the Fourth Book of Kings is equivalent to 2 Kings in the King James Version</note>, that they served Baal, and religiously passed their sons and daughters through his fire, in which they were imitated by the idolatrous Israelites. We also read in the same book, that Achar <note>Likely King Ahaz, who is recorded in 2 Kings 16:3 as "making his son pass through the fire"</note> king of Israel is blamed for having religiously passed his son through the sacred pagan fire; and it may be inferred from the said passage that many Israelitish kings provoked God, by the same idolatrous practice.

# AN ENQUIRY INTO THE FIRST INHABITANTS OF IRELAND.

The name of ***Nimrod*** <note>original: "Nembrod"</note> given also to **Belus**, which according to **Isidore** <note>Isidore of Seville, a 7th-century scholar whose "Etymologies" was a standard reference in the Middle Ages</note> literally signifies "tyrant" <note>original: "Nembrod tyrannum significat. Etym. l. 17."</note> can more naturally, and more in line with ancient mythology, be investigated and explained through the **Irish language**. It is a compound of *nem* (heaven) and *brod* (captivity), so that both words joined together as a title for **Belus**, the grandson of **Ham** <note>original: "Cham"</note>, plainly signify "captivator of heaven," or "conqueror of the heavens" <note>original: "Cæli captivator, or Cælorum expugnator"</note>. The Greek and Latin poets who describe the war of the giants against the gods use no stronger expression to paint the insolence of the former than "they wished to conquer the heavens" <note>original: "Cælos expugnare volebant"</note>. **Homer**, in his first *Iliad*, introduces **Venus**, who reminds **Jupiter** of her services in delivering him from his captivity and chains through her influence on the giant **Briareus** <note>In Greek myth, a hundred-armed giant who helped Zeus (Jupiter)</note>.

Besides this argument, it further appears from the collective authority of several learned commentators on the first book of **Genesis**, that **Belus** did not have the epithet **Nimrod** given to him until the time of his impious undertaking in building the tower <note>The Tower of Babel</note>. This act brought down upon him and his accomplices the immediate vengeance of God, not only through the total destruction of that building, but also by a multiplication of the first language into several dialects—though these were all still understood by the three sons of **Noah**. <note>original: "Isidor. loco supra citato." (Isidore, in the place cited above)</note>

Now, if we consider **Belus** after this harmful undertaking, and his seduction of so many thousand people into idolatry and rebellion against God, we will readily conclude that he was the only person living who most

If the **Phoenicians** <note>original: "Phenicians"</note> came from **Spain** to **Ireland**, it is probable they first planted themselves in the southern parts of the island; accordingly I find some plausible reasons to think their chief settlement must have been in a large district of the **county of Cork**, which comprehends the entire barony of **Fermoy** and the half barony of **Condons**. This district was anciently called ***Magh-Feine***, literally meaning the **plains of the Phoenicians** <note>original: "Phænio-magus"</note>; the inhabitants were always called ***Feara Maigh Feine*** <note>Men of the Plain of the Phoenicians</note>, afterwards the word ***Pheine*** was left out, as making the name too tedious, and only a part of the compound preserved by the moderns, who to this day call it ***Feára-maigh***, in English **Fermoy**.

The ***Book of Lecan*** <note>original: "Liber Lecanus," a medieval Irish manuscript containing genealogical and historical material</note> calls the inhabitants ***Fir Magh Feine***; the author or compiler of the ***Annals of Innisfallen*** <note>A 13th-century chronicle of Irish history</note>, at the year of Christ 254, mentions that **Fiacha Muillethan**, provincial king of **Munster** <note>original: "Momonian prince"</note>, bestowed this country called ***Magh-Feine*** to the celebrated druid **Mogruth**; but the author of the **Ulster book**, in the ***Book of Lecan***, is more exact; he mentions that in consideration of this druid's advice and influence over **Cormac** son of **Conn of the hundred battles** <note>Cormac mac Airt, a legendary High King of Ireland</note>, to give hostages to **Fiacha Muillethan**, after **Cormac's** signal defeat at the battle of **Dun Claire**, the **Munster** <note>original: "Momonian"</note> prince gave him and his posterity for ever, the lands called ***Magh mac Neirce***, which was afterwards called ***Dal-Mog-ruith***, and formed only a part of ***Magh-Feine***; It runs thus in verse:

Besides the affinity, or rather similarity of names, there are still to be discovered in the same district other plain monuments of **Canaanite** <note>original: "Chanaanitic"</note> or **Phoenician** <note>original: "Phenician"</note> fashion, such as are described by **Dom Calmet** <note>Antoine Augustin Calmet (1672–1757), a French Benedictine monk and renowned biblical scholar</note> in his learned comments on the **Pentateuch** <note>the first five books of the Bible</note>; I mean, large pillars of rude stone perpendicularly erected either separately, or joined with others in squares and circles, whereof some are placed as supporters to flat stones of a surprising magnitude, either in an inclined or horizontal position. It is indeed remarkable, that on the summit of many high places round the country of **MAGH-PHEINE**, we find heaps of stones joined together, with a huge stone on the top as a platform, whereon, it is probable, the builders immolated their victims, and lighted their sacred fires in honour of **Belus** <note>A name for the deity Baal, often associated with sun-worship in Phoenician and Middle Eastern cultures</note>. These without doubt were of the same nature with the high places of the children of **Canaan** or the **Phoenicians**, who communicated the use of them to the rebellious Jews, for which they are so frequently and so severely reprimanded by **Almighty God**.

No woman's name is more common in Ireland, among the old natives, than that which was **Dido's** proper **Punic** <note>The language of ancient Carthage, which was a Phoenician colony</note> or **Phoenician** name, according to **Solinus** <note>Gaius Julius Solinus, a 3rd-century Roman author known for his "Collection of Remarkable Facts"</note> and others; I mean **ELISSA**, a word which the old Irish, according to the character of their language, have shortened into **ELIS**, just as they have shortened the masculine name **DARIUS** into **DAIRE**. NATIONAL antiquities have always engaged the attention of every learned and polished people. The inquiries of (a) Psammitichus <note>Psammitichus I was an Egyptian Pharaoh (reigned 664–610 BCE); according to legend, he conducted an experiment to find the "original" human language by raising children in total silence.</note> into the original language, whatever truth may be in the anecdote, shows that such disquisitions <note>Formal discussions or investigations.</note> were not unusual in former times, nor in the flourishing periods of antient <note>archaic spelling of "ancient"</note> empires.

Questions involving national honour naturally raise the flame of patriotism in every breast, and produce contests between kingdoms concerning their antiquities. Intemperate zeal led to the fabrication of fictitious annals, and the wild (b) delusions of romantic history. These excesses created a

# ON THE STUDY OF

contempt of the learning, the customs and manners of remote times. When civilization was perfected, and found knowledge and just criticism enabled mankind to form proper discriminations, reason soon recovered her equilibrium; a calm review of these subjects succeeded, and the progress of mankind from barbarism to civility, through all the subordinate details of the arts and sciences, became, as it ever will be, the object of manly and rational investigation; and as a favourite study, which it is at this day, found universal countenance and encouragement.

It was the love of glory and of his country, traits the most conspicuous in the character of king Henry VIII. that dictated to him the appointment of *(c)* Leland <note>John Leland (c. 1503–1552) was an English scholar often described as the "father of English local history and bibliography."</note> to the office of <term>Royal Antiquary<gloss>An official post created to search for and preserve historical records and monuments following the dissolution of the monasteries.</gloss></term>. The consequence was happily decisive, in collecting a *(d)* body of men of real learning, and preserving from impending destruction, innumerable and valuable literary monuments. Much more might have been done, did not an undefined system of government, the pride of feudal grandeur, the intrusions of popular importance and religious heats <note>A reference to the intense religious and political conflicts of the Reformation and the subsequent century.</note>, disturb the reigns of queen Elizabeth and her successors: these excited groundless jealousies of their subjects, and made them apprehend *(e)* danger from the examination of a worm-eaten missal <note>A book containing the texts used in the Catholic Mass.</note> or a musty charter <note>A legal document granting rights, land, or privileges.</note>. These political terrors vanished at the Revolution <note>The "Glorious Revolution" of 1688, which shifted power from the monarchy toward Parliament.</note>, but it was not before the accession of the illustrious house of Brunswick <note>The House of Hanover, which began its reign in Great Britain with George I in 1714.</note>,

# IRISH ANTIQUITIES.

that antiquities, with every useful and ornamental branch of knowledge, received regal patronage. It was in the reign of king George II. that the society of antiquaries of London was incorporated.

To the animated exertions of individuals, and the munificent protection of a few noblemen and gentlemen, is England indebted for that great body of antient records and documents to be found in the British museum, and in the other public and private libraries of that kingdom. Unfortunately for Ireland, the same service was not rendered to her by any of her sons, except in a partial degree. A prey to the merciless ravages of the <term>Ostmen<gloss>A term used in medieval Ireland for the Vikings, or "men from the east," who established coastal settlements and raided inland monasteries.</gloss></term>; plundered by the <note>(*f*) English adventurers</note>; convulsed and torn by domestic broils; she shared every vicissitude of human misery, and preserved small remains of that piety and learning which made her the mart of literature and the admiration of Europe in the eighth and ninth centuries <note>This refers to the "Golden Age" of Ireland, when Irish monasteries were the primary centers of learning for Western Europe.</note>.

To retain the natives in their faith, the ecclesiastics, of the <term>Romish profession<gloss>A historical term for the Roman Catholic Church.</gloss></term>, collected and published the lives of their <note>(*g*) saints</note> and collateral historical pieces. In these, and in the subsequent defence of them, many points of national antiquities were discussed and ascertained. Manuscripts were searched for and carefully examined, copies of them multiplied, and a foundation was laid for the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the history of Irish scholarship. ->ON THE STUDY OF<-

...elegant superstructure built by Sir James Ware <note>Sir James Ware (1594–1666) was an Anglo-Irish historian whose systematic approach to records laid the foundation for modern Irish history.</note>. It is not easy to determine whether he deserves more praise for the clear division of his work or for the accuracy and depth with which he treats his subject. The entire project demonstrates a natural talent for such studies and a remarkable display of ability and scholarly knowledge.

Yet, this work must still be considered a pioneering but early effort, and it is very far from exhausting the various topics it covers. Neither the genius nor the hard work of any single man is enough to thoroughly explain the antiquities of a country; the additions made to the works of Camden <note>William Camden (1551–1623), an English antiquarian whose work *Britannia* set the standard for regional history.</note> and Ware prove this. Human talents are so varied, and success is so much more likely when a person follows their natural interests, that we are certain to find both information and entertainment when cleverness and scholarship unite to handle a single subject. To encourage such efforts and to gather the scattered insights of science, societies have been formed throughout Europe. 

Ireland has had her own <term>physico-historical<note>Societies in the 18th century that combined the study of natural history (the "physical") with historical research.</note></term> and antiquarian societies. Under the leadership of the former, histories of the counties of Down, Waterford, Cork, and Kerry have been published; and under the latter, the excellent works of Lieutenant-Colonel Charles Vallancey <note>Charles Vallancey (c. 1721–1812) was an engineer and amateur historian known for his enthusiastic—if often eccentric—theories on the Eastern origins of the Irish people.</note>. These early successes gave hopeful signs of progress, but a deadly lack of interest has so far withered the finest fruits of Irish literature; it remains a stranger to the encouraging support of either public or private patronage. While every other nation in Europe is refining the study of its ancient history and making new discoveries, Ireland—though full of learned men and historical curiosities of every kind—remains to the naturalist and the historian an <term>unknown land<note>original Latin: "terra incognita"</note></term>, a 
region

# IRISH ANTIQUITIES.

...region unexplored. A few sparks of patriotic passion are alone wanting to show its hidden treasures and elevate it to a rank it has always justly claimed, but never enjoyed. Undismayed by this look back at the past, the gentlemen joined together in the present work are happy to contemplate a taste and spirit now prevalent, very different from those of former ages. They observe with what eagerness foreign antiquarian research is read; nor can they pay their countrymen so poor a compliment, or show such a lack of confidence in themselves, as to imagine that their labors will be passed over with indifference or neglect.

As it has fallen to the lot of the writer to defend the studies of his colleagues and to address the public on this occasion, he humbly begs their indulgence for the following pages. These are designed to show what has and may be done toward the illustration of Irish antiquities; and which, for greater clarity, are organized under distinct titles.

## BREHON LAWS.

Barred from every intercourse with the natives by the most punishing laws <note>The "penal statutes" mentioned here refer to the various laws enacted by the English to restrict the rights and influence of the native Irish and Catholics.</note>, the English knew but little of their legal systems and municipal regulations. Consequently, every mention of their laws is expressed in terms of detestation. As the seizure of property <note>Original: "forfeitures"</note> was no part of the Brehonic institutes <term>Brehon Laws<note>The ancient native legal system of Ireland, which relied on professional jurists known as Brehons to arbitrate disputes and manage social order.</note></term>, the English—who were constantly enriching themselves through the legal offenses of the natives—without doubt condemned a system so unfavorable to their schemes. Without examining it closely, they pronounced it a "lewd custom" <note>In the 18th century, "lewd" often meant ignorant, unlearned, or base, rather than its modern sexual connotation.</note>.

### DRUIDIC LITERATURE.

# MODES OF INTERMENT.

The various modes of burial <term>sepulture: the act of burial or the state of being buried</term> practiced by our ancestors form a pleasing and extensive department in our antiquities. Sir James Ware <note>Sir James Ware (1594–1666) was a foundational figure in Irish history and antiquarianism; his works documented Ireland's medieval past.</note> has (a) treated it superficially; but (b) Mr. Harris <note>Walter Harris (1686–1761) was an Irish historian who significantly expanded and updated the works of Sir James Ware.</note>, with indefatigable industry, has made many valuable additions. The following remarks are given to excite attention, and as a specimen of what hereafter may be executed on a larger scale.

**First Epoch.** Wormius <note>Ole Worm (1588–1654), a Danish physician and antiquary, renowned for his study of runes and Scandinavian history.</note> has made a triple division of the modes of interment; it is certainly not accurate, but whatever contributes to throw a confused subject into order has its use, and must elucidate it. Cremation he speaks of first. Burning the body after death was universally prevalent in Europe in the earliest ages. It was (c) confessedly so in Ireland. There is a very concise, but objectionable, manner of accounting for this and other oriental customs, by saying, they were introduced by Phoenician <note>The Phoenicians were ancient maritime traders from the Levant, often credited by 18th-century scholars—sometimes erroneously—with bringing Eastern culture to Ireland.</note> colonies or Carthaginian traders. We can, in the present instance, recur to other and more satisfactory evidence.

Odin, the great legislator and deity of the northern nations, arrived in Europe, with his Asiatic Goths, (d) twenty-four years before the Christian era;

In Wormius's age of cremation, the body reduced to ashes was placed in an urn and laid in the earth, over which a (h) conical mound was raised, just sufficient to indicate what was under it. The urn is always of baked clay, and the mouldings round the rim often show both taste and design. Whatever we may conceive of the barbarism of those ages, they were by no means so rude and ignorant as is generally imagined; we have (i) classical authority that the Celts used earthen dishes at their tables, and pottery seems to have been well understood. ## IRISH ANTIQUITIES.

### SECOND EPOCH.
This <term>Wormius</term><note>Ole Worm (1588–1654), a Danish physician and antiquary who wrote extensively on Scandinavian monuments.</note> calls the age of hillocks; when grandeur and more labored sepulchres were formed, of considerable <margin>(*k*) By heaping up sand and earth until they rose to the proper height of a hillock.—They also surrounded the mounds themselves, both at the summit and around the base, with stones of remarkable size.—The second age, in which they placed the bodies whole and unburnt with their ornaments within a circle made of larger stones. <note>original: "Arenam et terram exaggerando... Worm. supra."</note></margin> height and surrounded with rows of stones. If the laws of <term>Odin</term><note>The chief god of Norse mythology; here referring to the legendary legal and cultural codes attributed to him regarding funeral rites.</note> were observed, the practices of the first and second epochs were the same. <term>Wormius</term> grounds the distinction on the bodies being consigned to the earth whole in the latter, which had it been regular, made a material difference; but skeletons and urns full of burnt bones are frequently found in the same cemetery, which proves the impossibility of defining the duration of a custom, when it is fluctuating and about to give way to a new one. The time when burning was exchanged for burying in Ireland, was, according to our historians, in the reign of <term>Eochadh</term><note>Eochadh Feidleach, a legendary High King of Ireland.</note>, fourteen years before <term>Christ</term>. There is reason to believe, cremation was not diffused for many years after, as <term>Pomponius Mela</term><note>A Roman geographer of the 1st century AD.</note>, who lived in the first century of <term>Christianity</term>, tells us the <term>Druids</term> used both modes of interment. So that probably not before the full establishment of the <term>Christian</term> religion in this isle did cremation entirely cease. It was again revived during the domination of the <term>Ostmen</term><note>The "Eastmen" or Vikings/Norsemen who settled in Ireland.</note> here, in the ninth and tenth centuries, who were then pagans. On account of this last circumstance, it is no easy matter to ascertain the date of those monuments.

<summary>This page describes the 'Second Epoch' of Irish burial practices, characterized by the transition from cremation to the interment of whole bodies within large earthen mounds or hillocks. It notes the difficulty in dating these monuments due to overlapping pagan and Christian customs, as well as the later influence of Viking (Ostmen) traditions.</summary>

The old oriental custom becoming unfashionable in this second epoch, a new style in the construction of sepulchres <note>tombs or burial vaults</note> and disposing of the body, began. While cremation continued, an elegant bell-shaped <note>original: "campani-form"</note> hollow artificially made in the earth, as the barrows at Stonehenge are, and covered by a light mound <term>tumulus: an ancient burial mound</term>, seemed a suitable receptacle for an urn and its ashen <note>original: "cineritious"</note> contents. This was the idea of the Romans, as appears by their <margin>Viaggiana, page 113.</margin> **Columbaria** <note>Subterranean sepulchres with niches for urns, resembling dovecotes.</note>. Vast heaps <note>original: "coacervations"</note> of clay and stones were esteemed proper and graceful for the new mode. The tombs of the greatest leaders at first were humble, but in process of time, says <margin>With the progress of time, more labor seems to have been bestowed on the tombs of the great. See above. <note>original Latin: "Ætatis progressu, plus operæ in magnatum tumulis positum videtur. Supra."</note></margin> **Wormius** <note>Ole Worm (1588–1654), a Danish physician and antiquary known for his study of runic inscriptions.</note>, more labour was bestowed on them.

The splendour of personal valour, of great exploits and extensive conquests, will ever give a brilliancy to the memory of illustrious princes, and create a profound veneration for them. **Odin** gave the names of his twelve <margin>Sturleson. <note>Referring to Snorri Sturluson, the Icelandic historian.</note></margin> deified companions to his children, to perpetuate this supernatural respect. From these the northern kings <margin>From whom (Woden) the royal lineage of almost all barbarian nations draws its line. William of Malmesbury. <note>original Latin: "De quo (Wodeno) omnium fere barbarorum gentium regium genus lineam trahit. Guil. Malmeſb."</note></margin> traced their pedigree. Divine honours were paid to them, and the places of their interment became places of worship. In the Larariums <note>private shrines for household gods</note>, the Romans preserved the ashes and pictures of their ancestors along with their household Gods. But since from the earliest origin everyone had the corpses buried within their own houses; from which the religious belief persuaded them that the Lares <note>guardian spirits of the home</note> should be worshiped in each house, in the Lararium, in which besides the household gods, they also had the fashioned images of distinguished men whom they might venerate. Wormius, Mr. Pennant, the Count de Caylus, and every other intelligent writer, allow these stone circles to be sepulchral <note>related to burial</note>; and that they certainly are sepulchral appears from a human body being found under each upright stone, in some circles lately opened in Connaught. So that after all that has been written on Stonehenge, it may be the sepulture <note>burial place</note> of Hengist <note>a legendary 5th-century Germanic leader</note> and the Anglo-Saxon chiefs; the first being buried under the altar, and the others under the trilithons <note>structures consisting of two large vertical stones supporting a third stone set horizontally across the top</note>.

These stone circles thus dedicated to religious uses, their application to other solemn purposes easily followed. Here kings were elected and inaugurated, and courts of justice and single combats were held. We had formerly in our <margin>(q) Canons of the church of Ireland, A. D. 1634. Canon 92.</margin> churches, plays, feasts, courts-leet <note>local manorial courts</note> and musters <note>military assemblies</note>, and at present our kings are anointed there, all remains of ancient usages. The rule given by Wormius, <margin>(s) Those which are surrounded at the base by a single or multiple row of stones are believed to be dedicated to the commanders of armies and other great men. —Wormius <note>original: "Ex iis qui una vel multiplici faxorum serie circa basim cinguntur exercituum imperatoribus, aliisque magnatibus dicati creduntur." The cairn <note>A mound of rough stones built as a memorial or landmark, often over a burial site.</note> as well as the stone pillar came from the east, and Rowlands, Harris and others are right in asserting it. Some are surrounded at the base with circles of stones, and some round the top; others have a circlet of smaller cairns on the summit of larger ones. As they did not require so much of exertion as of perseverance, stones everywhere abounding, there are a great number and variety of them. These piles, says Mr. Pennant, <note>Thomas Pennant (1726–1798), a Welsh naturalist and traveler.</note>

### IRISH MONASTIC RECORD. <note>original: "Monasticon Hibernicum"</note>

In a country where the clergy were as numerous as <margin>Nicolson's Irish Historical Library, preface page 14.</margin> all the other classes of men, and those for the most part Monks, an account of their orders and their various establishments includes no small share of the general history of the nation. About the middle of the fourth century Monachism <note>The system of living as a monk in a secluded religious community.</note> was introduced into Italy, and quickly spread through the West. The <margin>Found in Ussher, *The Beginnings of the British Churches*, chapter 16.</margin> biographers of St. Patrick tell us, he learned monastic discipline under Martin bishop of Tours; a prelate who had no less than two <margin>Sulpicius Severus, *Letter to Bassula*.</margin> thousand Monks in a monastery contiguous to his cathedral. Our apostle studied this fashionable profession for many years among the Italian ascetics, and his predilection for it was conspicuous when he formed the Irish church. In succeeding centuries these foundations multiplied amazingly, so that at the reformation, there were five hundred and twenty-nine monasteries and nunneries in this kingdom.

The *Irish Monastic Record*, the writings of Ware, Allemande and others, are too short to give satisfaction on so ample a subject. Doctor King archbishop of Dublin, who knew the value of it, did not think it beneath his attention to make large <margin>They are now in the library of the Dublin Society.</margin> compilations from original records for

The collecting and digesting of these documents, and procuring other materials, have employed for some years the leisure hours of a reverend clergyman and an associate. An extraordinary skill in our antiquities, and an eminence in every branch of polite learning, recommended him to the patronage of his late excellent diocesan <note>The bishop of a specific district or diocese.</note>, Doctor Pococke, successively bishop of Ossory and of Meath: than whom a better judge of merit, or a more generous protector of it, our hierarchy has not produced.

Sir William Dugdale and Mr. Dodsworth acquired immortal fame by the *English Monasticon* <note>original: "Monasticon Anglicanum"; a massive 17th-century work documenting the history of monasteries in England.</note>. The civil, military, and ecclesiastical history of Britain has derived, as Bishop Nicolson remarks, vast advantages from it: scarce a family of any antiquity or property but find themselves interested in it. The same would be the case in Ireland, did public spirit encourage a similar undertaking. Both the British and Irish libraries supply abundant matter. All that can be offered, under the present circumstances, are short annals and extracts from charters, which will form part of this *Collectanea* <note>original: "Collectanea de Rebus Hibernicis"; a collection of miscellaneous writings on Irish history.</note>.

## DOMINICAN IRELAND;

Or, a History of the Irish Province of the Order of Preachers <note>The Order of Preachers is the formal name for the Dominican Order, founded by St. Dominic in the 13th century.</note>, drawn from ancient manuscripts, approved authors, original letters never before printed, authentic instruments and archives, and other monuments of invincible faith. In which, not only all things pertaining to the mentioned province and its Monasteries, established both within and without the kingdom of Ireland (with the genealogies of their individual founders interspersed), and its alumni—distinguished either by episcopal dignity, the office of Provincial, the publication of books, martyrdom, or a public reputation for virtue—are succinctly and distinctly presented. But also many things regarding the regulars <note>Members of religious orders living under a "rule," such as monks or friars.</note> taken generally, the secular clergy, and even the civil affairs of Ireland and Great Britain, are appropriately inserted in various places with the addition of suitable notes, and arranged in a clear order.

After so large a title-page it is only necessary to observe, that it bears marks of considerable learning and industry. It was printed in Kilkenny under his own inspection, though the title mentions Cologn <note>Cologne, Germany; it was common for Irish Catholic books to list a foreign city on the title page to avoid legal trouble under the Penal Laws.</note> in Germany; in 1772, he published an appendix to this work. The particular notice taken of this book is, to recommend it as a pattern for other religious communities to imitate; and as one easy and obvious mean towards perfecting our <term>Monasticon</term> <note>A reference to a "Monasticon," a systematic catalog or history of monasteries and religious houses.</note>. Were the original charters and descriptions of the abbies, (some of them beautiful and well preserved) given, his work had been complete.

## ARCHITECTURE.
The buildings of the ancient Irish were the same as those of every uncivilized people; consisting of materials the slightest and the easiest to be procured. Sir James Ware <note>A 17th-century historian and pioneer of Irish antiquarian studies.</note> and his followers have fully stated this evidence, and there dropped the subject, leaving an impression upon the reader as if there were no other structures in Ireland but straw-built cottages. This, among other things, has supported that idea of barbarism so constantly instilled by books of geography and travels; and is, in reality, as glaring a falsehood as any to be found in such injudicious, flimsy, and wretched productions. It is certainly not amiss to return to the earliest customs of nations, but why stop there? Is it not as pleasing an investigation to trace the progress of knowledge from its simplest exertions to its most refined energies? The different stages of architecture mark, in decisive characters, the state of civility <note>In this context, "civility" refers to the level of social and cultural development or civilization.</note>: nor is there one topic in the antiquities of a kingdom productive of more elegant entertainment or more useful information.

In the following dissertation, the style of our ancient habitations and the dawnings of its improvement are given from apparently indisputable authority.

## ECCLESIASTICAL HISTORY.

<margin>(x) In this collection of ours, gathered indiscriminately from every kind of writer, etc. Preface to the Antiquities of the British Church. <note>original: "In nostra autem hæc ex omni scriptorum genere promiscue congesta farragine, &c. Præfat. ad Antiquit. Britan. Eccles."</note></margin>

### IRISH ANTIQUITIES.

O'Halloran <note>Sylvester O'Halloran (1728–1807), an Irish surgeon and antiquarian</note> thinks our apostle <note>Saint Patrick</note> might ordain ninety-six bishops, and as many of these might have died during his long life, and so many succeeded in their room, as completed the number above.

All this is mere conjecture: the solution of the difficulty arises from considering the state and practices of the early Christian church. From Clement of Rome <note>Clemens Romanus, an early Pope and Apostolic Father</note> we learn, that in his time, the episcopal order was very numerous; every <margin>Through regions and cities <note>original Greek: Κατα χωρας και πολεις</note>, Epistle to the Corinthians. Salmasius, *On the Primacy*. Burchard, book 1, chapter 125. Doctor Hammond says, *chora* <note>Greek: χωρα</note>, signifies a province. *Vindication of Episcopacy*, page 134, and from that infers they were not so numerous: but it will be seen from the nature of the episcopal office, and the custom of the oriental church, that his conclusion is not just.</margin> city, village, and region had a bishop; many cities had two and some more. Thus, <margin>Eusebius, Socrates, and Posidonius, *Life of Augustine*, chapter 8.</margin> Alexander and Narcissus were bishops of Jerusalem together; Paulinus and Miletus of Antioch; Theodosius and Agapetus of Synada, and Valerius and Augustine of Hippo. The custom for a city to have but one bishop, was first begun in Alexandria, as Epiphanius remarks. So much had the <term>chorepiscopi</term> <note>From the Greek for "country bishops," these were clergy who exercised episcopal functions in rural areas under the authority of a city bishop</note> or village-bishops increased, that it became necessary to restrain their number, which was done in the Laodicean council, in A.D. 367, and before in the Antiochean.

In the fourth century, St. Basil writes to Amphilochius, bishop of Iconium, that he did not approve of his multiplying bishops, as thereby the dignity went into contempt: it would be better, adds he, to choose some man worthy a bishopric, who might take priests to his assistance, than divide a small

<summary>This page discusses the historical prevalence of numerous bishops in the early Christian church to explain the high number of bishops reportedly ordained by Saint Patrick in Ireland. It cites early church fathers and councils to show that "village-bishops" were common before later restrictions were imposed.</summary>

These oriental practices were closely followed by St. Patrick. It is not insisted on, that the number he is said to have ordained, is accurate, but great as it is, it will not appear incredible.

By the fifty-eighth Neo-Cæsarean canon <note>A decree from the Council of Neocaesarea, an early Christian provincial synod held in modern-day Turkey</note>, made in A. D. 315, the administration of the sacraments were in a great measure, confined to the bishops and <term>chorepiscopi</term> <note>Country bishops who exercised episcopal functions in rural areas under the authority of a city bishop</note>; and in (e) Tertullian it is expressly declared, that <term>presbyters</term> <note>Priests or elders</note> had not the liberty of baptizing without the bishop's permission; or of preaching in some churches, as Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century Christian historian</note> assures us. Leo, in his ninety-second epistle, desires Maximus, bishop of Antioch, to hinder monks and laymen from preaching, which was solely the bishop's office. In England, by the third of archbishop Cuthbert's canons in A. D. 747, the bishop is enjoined to visit his parish once a year, and preach the word of God which it rarely hears. These last words refer as well to the paucity of clergymen then, as to the restriction of this duty to the bishop. Similar canons may be found in the legatine council of Cealchythe, in A. D. 785, and in Odo's, in A. D. 943.

The great offices of religion being thus confined to the bishops and their <term>suffragans</term> <note>Subordinate bishops who assist a metropolitan or diocesan bishop</note>, and that by the discipline of the eastern and western churches; we cannot be surprised at their increase, or at the number

### GREEK LANGUAGE.
Virgil <note>Also known as St. Vergilius of Salzburg, an Irish astronomer and bishop</note>, our countryman, was one of eight <margin>(i) Usher. I should wonder, says Usher, at a Grecian's going from Ireland, did I not know, that at Trim in Meath is a church called the Greek church, at this day, A. D. 1632. ### BROGUES WORN WITH HAY IN THEM.
At this day the practice is universal for the natives to have their brogues <note>A traditional Irish shoe made of untanned or "raw" leather.</note> stuffed with hay or straw, without being able to assign any reason for the custom: the following is offered both as curious and satisfactory. Linnæus <note>Carl Linnaeus (1707–1778), the famous Swedish botanist who formalized the modern system of naming organisms.</note> tells (o) us, the Laplanders <note>The Sámi people of northern Scandinavia.</note>, through their long and cold winters keep abroad with their herds of Rhen-deer <note>Reindeer.</note>: that they never experience kibes <note>Chilblains; painful swellings on the feet caused by exposure to cold and moisture.</note>, or other effects of intense frosts, by means of this precaution. In summer they cut and dry the slender-eared, broad-leaved cyperus grass, *carex vesicaria* <note>Commonly known as blister sedge.</note>; this they comb and rub in their hands, and then place it in their shoes so as to cover their feet and legs; their shoes are made of the skin of the Rhen-deer, the hairy part turned inward; they have no stockings, their breeches are of the same skin and come down to their ankles. This grass keeps off the cold in winter, and in summer prevents the effects of perspiration.

In like manner, an (p) eye-witness informs us, that the English, when they first settled in America, used the bastard *calamus aromaticus* <note>Sweet flag; a plant used for its medicinal and aromatic properties.</note> to keep their feet warm. The ancient brogue was made of raw, or half-tanned leather, of one entire piece, and gathered round the foot by a thong; such an one was found in the year 1770, at Aghaboe <note>A historic village in County Laois, Ireland, once a major monastic site.</note>, in a bog
<meta>seven</meta>

(p) Josselyn's New England rarities, page 53.

The encampments of these <term>Creaghts</term> are still to be seen in many parts of the kingdom; one of them is on Knockaolla Hill, near Castletown, in the Queen's county <note>Now known as County Laois.</note>. # A
## DISSERTATION
### ON THE
## ROUND TOWERS
### IN
## IRELAND.

Among the many curiosities of nature and art, with which this kingdom remarkably abounds, not one of them merits a more particular attention than the numerous, slender round towers <note>These are the iconic medieval stone bell-towers found throughout Ireland, characterized by their height and conical roofs.</note> everywhere found; and which, from the obscurity of their origin, and the uncertainty of their use, have opened to men of leisure and erudition <note>Scholarly learning or deep knowledge.</note>, a spacious field for hypothesis and conjecture.

It is worth remarking, that men blindly devoted to systems <note>In this context, "systems" refers to rigid historical theories or preconceived frameworks.</note> too often deal unfairly by the authors to whom they are most indebted; they select such parts as make for their argument; in these no error or imperfection appears; but those that contravene their favorite notions are reprobated as undeserving the least regard; not considering, that by invalidating the credibility and authority of a writer

<summary>This introductory page establishes the Round Towers of Ireland as a significant archaeological mystery and critiques the biased methods used by previous scholars to explain them.</summary>

# A DISSERTATION ON THE

writer in any instance, they must defeat the purpose for which they adduce him. Giraldus Cambrensis <note>Gerald of Wales (c. 1146–1223), a royal clerk and historian whose accounts of Ireland are foundational but controversial.</note> hath remarkably experienced this treatment; in some cases his testimony is alleged as decisive, when in others he is said to be fabulous and prejudiced; yet if we allow for the age in which he lived; the prevalence of romantic fiction; the low state of learning and criticism, with a tincture of personal vanity; we shall see him throughout his works, a man of excellent talents, and well versed in the various departments of science then known and cultivated. His coming to this kingdom at different times, with his continuance in it, must give a degree of credit to his relations not lightly to be regarded, nor rejected without sufficient reason.

> “The Irish nation, says <margin>This nation is, however, an inhospitable nation; a nation living only from beasts, etc. *Topography of Ireland*. <note>original: "Est autem gens hæc, gens inhospita; gens ex bestiis solum, &c. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the social and economic state of early Ireland, specifically addressing the transition to a pastoral society and the lack of industrial development as described by historical chroniclers.</meta>

# ROUND TOWERS IN IRELAND.

They had advanced to the shepherd-state, the second stage in the civilization of mankind; but their manners were little altered; their food, their domestication, and every other circumstance showed, that the liberty, the ferocity and untamed nature of tenants of the forest, were far from being reclaimed, and still farther from submitting to the salutary restraints of legal institutions. Their but little <margin>They affect civil wealth but little. <note>original: "Civiles gazas parum affectans."</note> Giraldus Cambrensis, as cited above.</margin> affecting civil wealth, (which includes the ornamental arts,) resulted from their want of foreign trade, home manufactures and great towns.

Cambrensis <note>Giraldus Cambrensis (c. 1146–1223) was a Cambro-Norman archdeacon and chronicler whose accounts of Ireland are foundational but often controversial due to his colonial perspective.</note> proceeds:—“The <margin>The different kinds of metals also, with which the interior veins teem, by the fault of that same idleness, neither come forth for use nor are they turned to profit. <note>original: "Metallorum quoque diverforum genera quibus venæ fœturiunt interiores, ejusdem ociositatis vitio, nec ad usum prodeunt, nec proficiunt."</note> Giraldus Cambrensis, as cited above.</margin> different kinds of metal with which the deeper veins abound, through an idle disposition are neither dug for any use, or turned to any account. For gold, which they covet to have in abundance, is imported among them by <term>Ostmen</term> <note>A term used for the Viking or Norse settlers who established coastal trading towns in Ireland.</note> merchants. They have no manufactures of linen, woollen, or other kinds. Sunk in indolence and sloth, they esteem it their highest bliss not to labour, and their greatest riches to enjoy their liberty.”—This and the former citation accord; there are an union and harmony of parts, which show the pencil of the painter was guided by truth in the great outline. It is scarcely possible to conceive, as it would be very preposterous to assert, that the writer rather indulged a licentious fancy on this occasion, than represented living manners; the most perverse nationality must blush to assert it.

->The<-

Coined money was not (f) common in the tenth century, among the northern nations, and out of the English pale <note>term: English Pale (the part of Ireland directly under English rule)</note> the Irish had very little.

<margin>(g) That for the rest, fines for redemption shall not be taken in cows, but in pence. <note>original: "Quod de cetero, fines de vaccis pro redemptione non capiantur sed denarii."</note> Prynne on the 4th Institute, 5th year of Edward III.</margin>

So that we may give full credit to his narration in this respect, notwithstanding what our antiquarians tell us of the vast treasures of our monarchs.

<margin>(i) Not even the use of cements was known among them. <note>original: "Ne cæmentorum quidem apud illos ufus."</note> Tacitus, *Germania*, chapter 16.</margin>
Cambrensis <note>Gerald of Wales, a 12th-century chronicler</note> having shown, that the dawnings of commerce among the Irish originated from their intercourse with the Ostmen <note>A term for the Viking settlers from the East, primarily Danes and Norwegians</note>, intimates also other arts, with which those foreigners brought them acquainted, and particularly that most useful one of masonry. The Celtic nations did not know (i) the use of lime or cements, and the reason is obvious; they were in a rude and dispersed state, without fixed habitations or domestic improvements; and timber abounded for every purpose. In America most of the public and private buildings are of wood, as well as the small fortresses in remote parts. It was the scarcity of timber, and the danger of wooden houses from fire in populous towns, that made mankind turn their attention to more durable materials, and borrow the arts of more civilized nations.

Cambrensis <note>Giraldus Cambrensis, a 12th-century chronicler</note>, in the preceding extract, accurately distinguishes the different sorts of Danish fortresses; some were of clay, others of lime and stone. The *fossatum* <note>Latin: a ditch or moated embankment</note> was generally a conical rising ground; the first intrenchment was made round the vertex <note>the highest point or summit</note>, and the earth thrown down the hill; this swelled its circumference and enlarged its base. By working thus, the ditches were easier made, and the *valla* <note>Latin: ramparts or palisades</note> or ramparts became higher and more precipitous. The exceptions are very few, and the church of St. Kianan <note>St. Cianán of Duleek; his church is traditionally cited as the first stone church in Ireland, giving the place its name 'Damhliag' or 'house of stone'</note>, built in the sixth century, is the *first instance* of any stone-work erected in this kingdom. They had no cities or towns in the earlier ages. In a country where the inhabitants have but few mechanical arts; where they draw most of their necessaries from the soil they cultivate, and where precious metals are not made equivalents, or signs of national wealth, there can be few or no cities. In their wars with the English they were at last obliged to avail themselves of the arts of their enemies, by erecting castles and other strong holds. This gave rise to stone-buildings in Leinster, Munster and Connaught, and soon after in Ulster. The northern bards <note>Traditional Gaelic poets and keepers of history</note> inveighed bitterly against this innovation, and represented it as a signal that the nation was ripening for foreign subjection.

> “Let us,” said one of them, “pull down those fortresses of the insidious enemy, and cease working for them, by erecting any of our own; their stratagems will assuredly wrest them out of our hands. Our ancestors trusted entirely to their personal valour, and thought the *stone-houses of the Galls* <note>original: "Galls" — a term used by the Irish to refer to foreigners, originally the Vikings and later the Normans/English</note> a disgrace to courage.”

Having evicted the Irish of any pretensions to works of lime and stone, the Ostmen <note>A term for the Vikings or "East-men" who settled in Ireland.</note>, who possessed this country for above a century and a half, appear to have first introduced them. On their conversion to Christianity in the (<gloss>w</gloss>) beginning of the tenth century, they expressed the most fervent and lively zeal for it, in founding churches, monasteries and religious structures; and so conspicuous and sincere were their lives, that at the English invasion they filled with reputation both bishopricks and abbies. From the proofs before alledged, it is evident that our ecclesiastical buildings were of wood, and therefore not calculated for bells, the common and useful appendage of such structures. The campanile <term>campanile: a bell tower, usually freestanding</term> or belfry in England was distinct from the church, as we learn from the Monasticon <note>A reference to "Monasticon Anglicanum," a major work on the history of English monasteries.</note>, and in Ireland it was the Round Tower. The following coincidences will confirm this opinion.
> “The era of the invention of bells, says (<gloss>x</gloss>) Mr. Bentham, is somewhat obscure; some traces of them may be discovered in our monasteries (<gloss>y</gloss>) even in the seventh century. That they are Danish works their round figure manifestly proves; this was the <margin>(_g_) Moreover, these camps were always round. <note>original: "Castra autem hæc semper rotunda."</note> Spelman, Life of Alfred, page 58.</margin> form of their camps and fortifications, of their barrows <note>ancient burial mounds</note> and stone circles, and of their ancient habitations still existing. <margin>(_h_) Olaus Magnus and Ole Worm, Danish Monuments.</margin> “It is unquestionably certain,” says <margin>(_i_) Critical Dissertations, page 293.</margin> the learned Dr. Macpherson, “that the oldest forts on the western and northern coasts of Scotland, were erected by the barbarians of northern Europe. All the Norwegian towers in the Ebudes <note>The Hebrides islands off the coast of Scotland</note> were of a *circular* form; the old square castles there are of a much later date.” — The opinion of a <margin>(_k_) Walsh’s Prospect, pages 416, 417.</margin> respectable native writer deserves notice: “It is most certain, that those high, round, narrow towers of stone, built cylinder-wise, whereof Cambrensis <note>Gerald of Wales, a 12th-century chronicler</note> speaks, were never known or built in Ireland, as indeed no more were any castles, houses, or even churches of stone, at least in the north of Ireland, before the year of Christ 838, when the heathen Danes possessing a great part of that country, built them in several places, to serve as watch-towers against the natives. Though ere long the Danes being expelled, the Christian Irish turned them to another, and much better, because a holy use, that is to steeple-houses or bell-fries; from which latter use made of them it is, that ever since to this present, they are called in Irish <term>*cloghteachs* (bell-houses)</term>, that is, bell-fries or bell-houses; *cloc* or *clog* signifying a bell, and *teach* a house in that language.”

# ROUND TOWERS IN IRELAND.

In an ancient Irish manuscript (says the <note>Charles Smith (1715–1762), a pioneering Irish topographer and historian.</note> historian of the county of Cork) containing some annals of Munster, there is mention made of the building the tower of Kineth <note>Now known as Kenneigh, County Cork; it is unique for having a hexagonal base.</note> in this county, about the year 1015, soon after the celebrated battle of Clontarf <note>A major battle in 1014 where High King Brian Boru defeated Viking forces.</note>. This is too late a period to make it the era of their general erection by at least a century; but it does not weaken the probability of both Danes and natives continuing the practice of building such structures until nearly the time of the Norman invasion, that is, until an improved taste in architecture took place, which was about that time. Mr. Brereton <note>Owen Salusbury Brereton (1715–1798), an English antiquary.</note> imagines these towers to be places from whence the people were called to worship by the sound of some wind instrument. This notion arose from an iron trumpet being found in one of them; if this circumstance be really true, it is a new proof of their being Danish works, though the inference deduced may be doubtful. The author last cited asserts, that these towers preceded the use of bells in Ireland; this is conjecture without the least proof; bells were used in the ceremonies of religion as early as the fifth century, and these towers were constructed about the tenth.

Whether there are any round towers in Denmark or Norway is not positively decided; we have no evidence of accurate inquiries being made after them; for cursory questions put to ignorant natives, or the reports of superficial observers will never determine <note>Catchword from the bottom of the original page to ensure continuity with the next.</note>

determine the point. Mr. Pennant <margin>(o) Tour in Scotland.</margin> tells us, that while he lay in the sound of Iona <note>The island of Iona, a famous center of Gaelic monasticism.</note> in the Hebrides, two gentlemen from the isle of Mull, and who had possessions there, seemed to know nothing of that astonishing curiosity, the Basaltic isle of Staffa <note>Staffa is famous for its unique hexagonal basalt columns, similar to the Giant's Causeway.</note>. If such was the want of attention and information in persons of property, and to so extraordinary a rarity in their vicinity, we may well suppose the round towers, if any there be abroad, are as indifferently passed over. Nor is it less remarkable, that until a mercurial <note>In this context, "mercurial" likely refers to a quick-moving or lively traveler.</note> <margin>(p) Twiss's Tour in Ireland, 1775.</margin> traveller enumerated those in this kingdom, they had been before but slightly noticed by our historians, topographers and antiquaries, though more than forty of them, and those well preserved, still remain. The same may be said of the Swedish antiquities, which were but imperfectly known until Dahlberg lately exhibited many of them in his *Sweden Ancient and Modern* <note>original: "Suecia Hodierna et Antiqua"</note>.

But the late reception of Christianity will very well account for the want of round towers, or other ancient monuments of religion, in northern Europe. The Normans, Danes, Swedes and their neighbours did not embrace <margin>(q) Spanheim. Historical Letters on the Church <note>original: "Epist. Hist. Eccles."</note> p. 444—501—502. William of Malmesbury <note>original: "W. Malmesb."</note> pag. 69.</margin> the doctrine of redemption until the twelfth century. At that time the more southern nations were tolerably civilized; sumptuous edifices dedicated to sacred uses, were common among them, the ancient being mouldered into ruins, or sunk into oblivion; even had they survived,

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins and architectural purpose of Irish round towers, specifically addressing their relationship to church steeples and their unique national character.</meta>

# ROUND TOWERS IN IRELAND.

survived, no one would dream of making them models of imitation.

After all, these structures might have been peculiar to Ireland, and Giraldus Cambrensis <note>A 12th-century chronicler also known as Gerald of Wales</note> intimates as much when he says, they were built *after the fashion of the country* <note>original: "patrio more"</note>. The circular Norwegian towers, mentioned above by Dr. Macpherson, might have suggested the idea of them, and their appropriation to religious purposes arose from the state of Christianity then in this kingdom. When once they were adopted, they soon were multiplied.

Mr. Pennant, speaking of the Scottish round towers, says they could not be intended for belfries, because they are placed near the steeples of churches, infinitely more commodious for that end. This supposes the steeples and round towers to be of the same age <note>original: "coeval"</note>, which is not the case; our ancestors, whatever may be thought of them at this day, were incapable of committing such a blunder <note>original: "solecism"</note> in architecture. Though the tower was used as a belfry, yet this by no means superseded the necessity of steeples on stone-buildings of later construction; such structures, being generally in the form of a cross, had a steeple raised on its intersection. Thus in the cemetery of the cathedral of St. Canice, Kilkenny, there is a beautiful round tower; yet the symmetry of the cathedral, which is Gothic, required a steeple, and it has one. Wherever we find a round tower, we may be certain the church contiguous to it is of early antiquity, and not later than the eleventh century.

<summary>The author argues that Irish round towers were unique national structures used as belfries before the introduction of Gothic steeples, dating the adjacent churches to no later than the eleventh century.</summary>

A learned <margin>Lieutenant Colonel Vallancey. *Collectanea*, Number 2, page 206.</margin> <term>antiquary</term> <note>A scholar of ancient history and artifacts, similar to a modern archaeologist.</note> is of the opinion that these towers were erected by the Phoenicians or Carthaginians in their trading voyages hither; some passages in ancient authors seem to countenance this conjecture. Diodorus Siculus, <margin>Book 2, page 91.</margin> <note>A Greek historian of the 1st century BCE.</note> discoursing of the Hyperboreans <note>A mythical people of the far north in Greek legend.</note> who inhabited an island opposite the Celtic region, says: “The trees there bear fruit twice every year. The fabulous chronicle records, that Latona <note>The mother of Apollo and Diana.</note> was born in that country; hence the inhabitants adore Apollo, whose praises they are continually chanting. In the isle is a noble forest, dedicated to this deity, a temple also of a spherical figure, filled with presents, and a city sacred to the same God; the greatest part of the people are musicians, they play on the harp in his temple, and sing hymns to his praise.”

This description is plainly the work of fancy; the author is not to be censured, when he himself intimates a suspicion of his authority.

Neither are they the <term>Pyratheia</term> <note>Fire-temples used in ancient Persian worship.</note> of the Persians, a notion lately taken up on the authority of Mr. Hanway, <margin>Travels into Persia, part 2, chapter 43.</margin> who in his travels into Persia, found round towers in the country of the Gaurs, or worshippers of fire. Mr. Hanway barely mentions their existence without reasoning on, or deducing any inference from it. In the year 634, the Saracens conquered Persia, and placed on its throne a monarch of their own. As to their being Phoenician works, we know these people derived much of their religion from <margin>Kircher, Oedipus Aegyptiacus, volume 1, page 216.</margin> Egypt; it is therefore to be presumed, that their temples resembled those of the latter, which consisted of four <margin>Labyrinth, Gateway, Antechamber, and Temple. The opinion acquiesced in for some years, is that of the late reverend dean Richardson, and since adopted and improved by Mr. Harris, in his edition of Sir James Ware's antiquities. This supposes our towers to be the residence of anchorite monks <note>Hermits who live in total seclusion</note>, and imitations of the eastern pillars; and is founded on the account Evagrius <note>A 6th-century church historian</note> gives of Simeon Stylites <note>A Christian ascetic who lived for 37 years on a small platform on top of a high pillar</note>, which, to avoid ambiguity, it may be proper to <margin>(z) extract:</margin> “The fabric of Simeon's church represents the form of a cross, beautified with porticoes of four sides; opposite these are placed pillars, curiously made of polished stone, whereon the roof is gracefully raised to an height. In the midst of these porticoes is an open court, wrought with much art, in which court stands the *pillar*, forty cubits high, whereon that incarnate angel upon earth (Simeon) led a celestial life.” It must require a warm imagination to point out the similarity between this pillar and our tower. The one was solid, the other hollow; the one was square, and the other circular.

The *workhouse of a narrow confinement* <margin>(a)</margin> <note>original: "arcti inclusorii ergastulum"</note> of Harris, was a monastic cell or hermitage. Mr. Harris <note>Walter Harris (1686–1761), an Irish historian and editor of Sir James Ware's works</note> speaking of Donchad O Brien, abbot of Clonmacnois, who shut himself up in one of these cells, says: “I will not take upon me to affirm, that it was in one of these towers at Clonmacnois he was enclosed.”
—Here was a fine opportunity of proving his hypothesis was it capable of it. Mr. Harris should have shown from history, or other monuments, that these round towers were the receptacles of anachorites <term>Anachorites: religious recluses or hermits who lived in total seclusion</term>, and not to have begged the question; but not a word of this is to be found in any record, either in print or manuscript; all he is able to produce is the names of twenty five ascetics, in the space of 503 years. D. 862, etc.” this surely hath no reference to, nor doth it prove them to have been inhabitants of the round towers. Thus destitute of every foundation, it is astonishing how implicitly and unreservedly this reverie <note>A fanciful or impractical idea</note> hath been embraced. What Lucretius <note>Titus Lucretius Carus, Roman poet and philosopher</note> observes of the wonders of nature is as applicable to literary paradoxes <note>original Latin: "Nil adeo magnum, nec tam mirabile quicquam Principio, quod non minuant mirarier omnes Paulatim."</note>:
Nothing is so great or so marvelous at first that everyone does not gradually cease to wonder at it.
