--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of various theories regarding the origins and functions of Irish round towers, specifically addressing the claims made by Charles Smith.</meta>

# A DISSERTATION ON THE

We are told by Mr. Smith <margin>History of the county of Cork, vol. 2. page 407.</margin> that he once believed those towers to be the retreats of anchorites <note>Hermits or religious recluses who live in total isolation.</note>; but from new information, derived from an Irish manuscript, he now thinks they were places of penance. The penitent mounted on the highest loft, performed there a probation of some days; in like manner he proceeded to the next stage, and so downwards until he finished his religious course, when he was received at the door of the tower, (which faced the western door of the church) by the clergy and people. And he credits this notion, as it coincides with the general position of the doors of these towers, which are always to the east; but the position is positively otherwise, as will presently be seen in those of Abernethy and Brechin, nor is any general rule observed in placing the doors, as he must have known from examining many of them in the counties he describes. It was therefore very disingenuous to sacrifice his veracity in support of a groundless hypothesis, and an anonymous manuscript, especially as he himself supplies the strongest proof of their original designation in <margin>History of the county of Waterford, page 71.</margin> another work, where he remarks, that the round tower of Ardmore had been used for a belfry <note>A structure or room in which bells are hung.</note>, there being towards the top, not only four windows to let out the sound, but also three pieces of oak, still remaining, on which the bell was hung. And Mr. Pennant informs us, the tower of Brechin is used for a belfry; as is that of Roscrea. This evidence seems decisive; it is truth confirmed by immemorial usage, and triumphing over learned whims.

->The<-

# ROUND TOWERS IN IRELAND.

The profound silence of Archbishop Ussher <note>James Ussher (1581–1656), Archbishop of Armagh and a famous scholar of biblical chronology and Irish church history.</note> and of Lynch, Roth, Ward, Colgan, O'Flaherty and O'Conor—men who have successfully elucidated Irish antiquities—is a negative argument against the numerous systems before noticed, not easily to be answered.

With the utmost diffidence of himself and profound respect for the learned, the writer of these pages does not think the aggregate of his inductions incontrovertible, nor the solution of this difficult problem perfectly satisfactory; he offers them as the result of much inquiry, and the conviction of his own mind on this obscure subject.

> “I went directly, says he, to Abernethy, the ancient capital of the Pictish nation, about four miles from Perth, to see if I could find any remains of the Picts <note>An ancient tribal confederation in what is now eastern and northern Scotland</note> hereabout; but could discover nothing except a stately hollow pillar without a staircase; so that when I entered within and looked upward, I could scarce forbear imagining myself at the bottom of a deep draw-well. It has only one door or entrance, facing the north, somewhat above the basis, the height of which is eight feet and a half, and the breadth from jamb to jamb, two and a half. Towards the top are four windows, which have served for the admission of light; they are equidistant, and five feet nine inches in height, and two feet two inches in breadth, and each is supported by two small pillars. At the bottom are two rows of stones, projecting from beneath, which served for a basis or pedestal. is forty-eight feet, but diminishes somewhat towards the top, and the thickness of the wall is three feet and a half. This is by the inhabitants hereabouts called the round steeple of Abernethy, and is supposed to be the only remains of Pictish <note>The Picts were a tribal confederation of people who lived in what is today eastern and northern Scotland during the Late Iron Age and Early Medieval periods.</note> work in these parts.”

However this has no pedestal like the other, but seems to shoot out of the ground like a tree; it has a door fronting the south; the height and breadth of which differ little from Abernethy; only upon it are evidences sufficient to demonstrate that it was a Christian work, for over the top of the door is the figure of our Saviour on the cross, with two little images or statues towards the middle.

“This steeple has a low spiral roof of stone, with three or four windows above the cornice, and on the top thereof is placed a vane. It has no staircase within, any more than the other, but the inhabitants of both towns ascend to the top by ladders. The vulgar <note>In this context, "vulgar" refers to the common or popular belief of the local people rather than something offensive.</note> notion of these is, that they are Pictish, and I should easily have rested in that opinion, had I not been since that time assured, that some of the like monuments are to be seen in Ireland, where the Picts never were settled.”

## SHEAN CASTLE.

### DUNAMASE AND SHEAN CASTLE.

In the Queen's County <note>Now known as County Laois</note>, the place commonly called Dunamase was originally said to be constructed by Laigfeach, a celebrated hero, and from him called—"Dûn uï Laigfeach, or the fortress of Laigfeach." It is not easy to understand the writer's meaning in this passage; having, probably, never seen this isolated rock, or only viewed it at a distance, he imagined it was a work of art <note>Meaning a man-made structure rather than a natural formation</note>; for the buildings on it are no more remarkable than similar ones of lime and stone. His ignorance also of the ancient Irish language makes him produce a very unsuitable and inadequate etymology; the common name, as he is pleased to style it, preserves a strong resemblance to the Celtic original, and leads us to its primitive designation.

Mr. Pennant, in his tour in Scotland, saw, near Struan, a Danish fortification on the top of a rock; about a furlong distant was another large fortified rock; these fortresses, he adds, are universally called, in the Erse <note>The Gaelic language of Scotland</note>, *Dûns*. There are two *Dûns* in the Queen's County, and they are the same in every circumstance as the Scottish ones; this one of Dunamase, and the other at Clopoke, about five miles distant. They are cone-shaped hills of limestone, exhibiting a very singular appearance, and were not only defensible by a small garrison, but before the use of artillery, almost impregnable.

On the submission of the Irish chiefs to King Henry the Second, the English government divided the country among the adventurers, as the only means to extend and retain its conquests. In the fragment of history given to us by <margin>Harris's *Hibernica*, page 41.</margin> Maurice Regan <note>A 12th-century interpreter and secretary to Diarmait Mac Murchada, King of Leinster</note>,

As it bounded the English pale <note>The "Pale" was the region of Ireland directly under the control of the English government in the Middle Ages.</note> on the west, a strong castle was built there to protect the vicinity; it was the residence of the (1) seneschal <note>term: seneschal (an administrative officer or steward representing the lord in legal and financial matters)</note>, who represented the lord; and the seat of military authority and civil jurisdiction; here all the incidents of the feudal system were discharged, and hither the tenants resorted for justice and protection. The precise time of erecting this castle cannot be ascertained, but it may be conjectured to have been about the beginning of Henry III's reign, in the year 1216; for nearly at the same time, the castle of Ley, eight miles distant, was erected by the barons of Ophaly, on the banks of the Barrow; a structure, in the thickness and height of its walls, its vaults and disposition of rooms, and its general style of building, resembling the former.

This gave rise to the numerous castles that surround Dunamafe; as Dysart, Palace, Shean, Moret, Ballymanus, Coolbanagher, Ballybrittas, Kilmarter and Ballyknockin.

Nor were the other concomitants of baronial magnificence wanting to Dunamafe. About it lay the demesne <note>Land kept by the lord for his own use rather than leased out.</note> and other tenemental lands; the Great-Heath was the lord's waste and common to the manors, and the castle was crowded with armed men, the terror of the neighbourhood, and the bulwark of the Pale <note>The region around Dublin under direct English rule.</note>. Such was the situation of Dunamafe for many years. While the British settlers preferred their original manners, the fickleness of the Irish, and their proneness to resistance were effectually curbed; but when the pride of power, without any of the virtue that acquired it, was only found among them; when corruptions had degraded their national character, they then became contemptible to those who formerly dreaded them, and instead of masters became suitors for protection.

On the retreat of Ormond, these forts (p) submitted to general Preston, but were re-taken by the king's forces, and continued in their possession until the year 1646, when Owen Roe O’Neil entered the Queen's county <note>Now County Laois</note>, committing every act of outrage and cruelty; he (q) took Dysart, Maryborough <note>Modern Portlaoise</note>, Cullenbrack, Sheehen alias Disiden, Bealaroyn, Castlereban, etc. Dunamase within a small mile of Dysart did not escape.

In 1648, O’Neil offered to surrender his garrisons in the Queen's county to colonel Jones, and to lay down his arms, provided he and the confederate catholics might have the privileges confirmed to them which they enjoyed in the reign of king James; but this was not accepted; the next year lord Castlehaven drove O’Neil out of the county.

In July 1650, Maryborough, Dunamase, and the neighbouring forts surrendered to the colonels Hewson and Reynolds; Dunamase was blown up and effectually dismantled, as were the rest.

On each side of the barbican <note>A fortified gateway or outwork of a castle.</note> were ditches, as far as the hill was accessible, and the (r) outward ballium <note>original: "ballium"; the courtyard or ward within the castle walls.</note> was flanked with two towers or bastions; the first gateway is seven feet wide, and the walls six feet thick; it has a (s) machicolation <note>An opening in the floor of a projecting gallery through which missiles or liquids could be dropped on attackers.</note> over it, for pouring down melted lead or scalding water; the wall of this ballium is a parapet, crenellated <note>Having battlements with gaps for firing.</note>, and to the N. E. is twenty feet high, with long chinks and oillet holes <note>Small circular openings at the end of arrow slits.</note>. The distance between tower and tower is one hundred and seventy-four feet.

Between the outward and inward ballia is a length of one hundred feet; the gate of the latter is placed in a tower, and over it was a guard-room; in the thickness of the walls are side passages admitting but one person at a time, and he by no means corpulent. From this second tower begins the parapet wall that surrounds the summit of the hill; its circumference is 1086 feet; the area is not perfectly circular, though nearly so, as far as the projections and inequalities of the rock will admit; so that its diameter at top is 362 feet. The hill is naturally an elliptical conoid <note>A geometric shape resembling a cone with an oval base.</note>; in some parts, from its base to its vertex, it measures 200 feet.

Probably, some have supposed this to be the chapel; it is oriented east and west, and the eastern window remains entire. It was this appropriation to a religious use <note>The author suggests that the building's identification as a sacred space protected it from the iconoclasm of later sieges, likely referring to the Cromwellian era.</note> that perhaps stopped the fury of the fanatic destroyers of this building and left it untouched. Contiguous to this was a dwelling house, seventy-two feet long and twenty-one wide; on this were platforms and embattled parapets, from whence the garrison might see and command the exterior works. The house was divided into apartments, and vaults ran under the whole. To the North-West was a well of excellent water; and on the west was what tradition calls a prison, but it seems to have been a poistern <term>poistern: a secondary door or gate, usually located in a concealed part of a fortification</term>. The naked rock appears on the North-East side, and the approach to the other parts was difficult and dangerous. When whole and complete, it was a beautiful model of military architecture, and even at this day presents the curious visitant with noble ruins of its former grandeur.

Small silver coins, belonging to the early Irish princes, have been found at Dunamase; there is one in the collection of the Reverend Mervyn Archdall <note>Mervyn Archdall (1723–1791) was a noted Irish antiquary and author of the 'Monasticon Hibernicum'.</note>, rector of Attier-Attanagh in the diocese of Ossory, that is a great curiosity; it is the size of a silver four-pence, but thinner; on the face is this epigraph—*Re Morrah*, King O’Mora, or O’More; and on the reverse is *Na Dunegh*, or rather *Dunadh*, of the Fort <note>original: "Na Dunegh, or rather Dunadh"</note>; expressing the place of coinage and the residence of the chief. The letters are the ancient Ogham croabh characters <note>Ogham is an Early Medieval alphabet used primarily to write the early Irish language; 'croabh' refers to the branch-like appearance of the script</note>, and the same with those that appear on the coins of O’Toole, found at Glendaloch in the county of Wicklow. ## SHEAN CASTLE,

As we before observed, was a manor dependent
on Dunamase <note>A prominent hill-fort and later Anglo-Norman stronghold in County Laois.</note>, and built not many years posterior
to it. The name is variously written. In a record

expresses its exposure to all the vicissitudes of wea-
ther, it standing on a high eminence and un-
sheltered.

In an account of the castles in the Queen's county
in 1615, it is called Shian; and on the banks of
the Blackwater near Lismore, in the county of
Waterford, is a Shian castle; but the <gloss>(u)</gloss> writer
who gives this information offers nothing in expla-
nation of the appellation.

It has escaped the notice of our antiquaries, that
the English on their settlement here, gave Saxonic
names to places, which have passed with the in-
curious for Irish ones, and therefore their etymology
is in vain sought for in the latter language; thus the
name of the third cantred <note>A medieval land division similar to a hundred.</note> of the barony of Ossory,
in the Queen's county, is Upperwoods; intimating
its forests mounted on its lofty mountains. The
parish,

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the fortifications of the Queen's County (Laois), specifically focusing on the relationship between the fortress of Dunamase and the neighboring Shean Castle.</meta>

<page-num>159</page-num>

## DUNAMASE AND SHEAN CASTLE.

The parish, which comprehends the whole <term>cantred</term><note>A medieval administrative division of land, similar to a barony or hundred.</note>, is called Offerelane, corrupted from Oferlyng, which in Saxon means superior, and is synonymous to Upperwoods. Shean might originally have been Sien, the Saxon *seen*, the pupil or sight of the eye, it bearing this analogy to Dunamase <note>The author suggests Shean acted as the "eye" or lookout for the larger fortress of Dunamase.</note>. The English spoke almost pure Saxon in the reign of Henry II, which gives countenance to the preceding conjecture.

Shean castle is situated on one of those high conical hills, which are so common in its vicinity. Though not remarkable for its magnitude, it was a place of considerable strength; the declivities round it being steep and easily defended. By the (w) record before cited, it appears, that Sir Robert Preston in the year 1397, held by the law of England, the inheritance of Margaret his late wife the manor of Sion in Liex <note>Leix, the modern County Laois.</note>, of Roger Mortimer, as of his manor of Dunmaske (Dunamase); it shared the revolutions of the latter in the subsequent periods of history; but being neither so strong or tenable it escaped demolition, and continued for centuries in its pristine state, until it came into possession of its present occupier, the reverend doctor Charles Coote, dean of Kilfenora.

He has revived Shean with new splendor, and added at a vast expence, such embellishments to its fine situation, as make it both an ornament to the country and a delightful residence.

(w) <note>original Latin: "Tenuit per legem Angliæ de hæreditate Margaritæ, nuper uxoris suæ, manerium de Sion in Leix, de Rogero de Mortuomari, ut de manerio suo de Donmaske."</note> He held by the law of England from the inheritance of Margaret, lately his wife, the manor of Sion in Leix, from Roger de Mortimer, as of his manor of Donmaske. *Hibernica*, page 74.

It has been much debated within the <term>Republic of Letters</term><note>A phrase used from the 15th to 18th centuries to describe the international community of intellectuals and scholars.</note> whether the ancient inhabitants of Ireland, before their conversion to the Christian faith, possessed the use of letters or any means of communicating their thoughts in writing. If we believe their antiquaries and historians, no people cultivated learning with such diligence, and that at a time when the Greeks and Romans remained in a state of barbarism. According to these authors <margin>O'Flaherty, Keating, Toland, Harris, etc.</margin>, the art of writing was known on this island as early as the tenth century before the Christian era. Volume II. era; for it is asserted, that Ollainfodlah <note>Ollamh Fodhla, a legendary high king and lawgiver of ancient Ireland</note> a king of Ireland, about the year of the world 3236, instituted the convention of Taragh <note>The Hill of Tara, the traditional seat of the High Kings of Ireland</note> in the county of Meath, as a college or society to inspect genealogies, chronicles and histories; whatever passed their scrutiny was inserted in a book or register, called the psalter of Taragh, which for several ages was considered as the grand repository of the archives of the kingdom. This convention however appears to have been the first, which publicly cultivated this excellent art; for it is acknowledged that writing was not in general use, and the Irish did not commit their poems and laws to writing till near 700 years after, that is, in the reign of M'Neffan <note>Conchobar mac Nessa</note> king of Ulster.

Notwithstanding the circumstantial account given by the antiquaries and historians of the middle and latter ages, relative to the learning and civilization of the ancient Irish, the learned in general have been much divided on this head; as the testimonies hitherto given, have rather been positive assertions, unsupported by proofs and matters of fact, than real histories. For though they have made frequent mention of several species of letters and alphabets, made use of by the pagan inhabitants of this island, yet they have given very few specimens of the characters, and none before their conversion to the Christian faith. And though they also make frequent mention of ancient writings or records, from which their more modern histories are supposed to be taken, few if any of these records have come down to our time. ->### DRUIDISM REVIVED.<-

To support the authenticity of their historical transactions, they have alleged that during the ravages committed by the Danes <note>The Viking invasions of Ireland beginning in the late 8th century.</note> in the ninth century, the greater part of the books and chronicles were destroyed. They further claim that the few remaining records were either destroyed or carried by the clergy to the continent during the contests with the English and the civil wars which rent this unhappy country for nearly 800 years. However, though diligent inquiry has been made by several learned persons in Spain, Denmark, and other countries, no such writings have hitherto been found.

Even the most authentic Irish records which have in any degree come within the reach of the world of letters—such as the *Annals of Ulster*, *Innisfail*, *Tigernach*, and the *Psalter of Cashel* <note>These are foundational medieval Irish chronicles; the author notes they were compiled between the 10th and 12th centuries.</note>—all of which were written about the tenth, eleventh, and twelfth centuries, begin their accounts at the fifth century and conclude with the tenth, without making the least mention of the pagan state of the Irish. If there had been any records relative to that period at the time of compiling these works, or any remarkable tradition handed down by the bards, we may reasonably suppose they would have mentioned it. 

Their silence on this head may serve in a great measure to confirm us in the general opinion of the learned: that the ancient inhabitants of Ireland did not have the use of letters prior to their conversion to the Christian faith. It suggests that the first alphabet this island ever saw was that of the Latins of the middle ages, introduced by St. Patrick or some other of the Christian missionaries. This silence also supports the assertions of most of the Greek and Latin writers, such as Strabo, Diodorus Siculus, Caesar, Pliny, and others. These

Seeing therefore the most respectable authorities, ancient and modern, agree in excluding not only the Irish, but all the Celtic tribes in general from an early knowledge of letters, it may be thought presumptuous to attempt the establishment of a contrary opinion. But we ought not to be deterred by the authority of great names, however respectable, from searching after truth; for though those whom we have quoted and many others, have declaimed against the use of letters among the aboriginal inhabitants of Europe, yet there are several who have maintained the contrary. Laertius <note>Diogenes Laertius, a 3rd-century biographer of Greek philosophers.</note> in his life of Aristotle, and in his discourse on philosophy, says, that the Greeks had the original of their theology, and the most sublime parts of their philosophy from the druids of the Celts (n). And J. Magnus <note>Johannes Magnus (1488–1544), a Swedish archbishop and historian.</note> asserts (o), that the northern inhabitants of Europe had the art of expressing things by writing, long before the Latins invented letters; and that the aboriginals of Italy, whom the Romans expelled on their settlement therein, though rude, had the knowledge of letters and taught them to their conquerors. Even the Goths in a very early period, engraved characters or letters on large stones,

The Goths had their own letters, evidence of which is provided by stones of extraordinary size; which, being inscribed with the forms of letters, might persuade one that they had been erected there by giant-like strength before the universal flood, or shortly thereafter. J. Magnus, Book 1, *History of the Swedes*, chapter 7.

From these testimonies it appears probable, that the Celtic, Sarmatic <note>Sarmatic: referring to the Sarmatians, ancient Iranian nomadic tribes who inhabited parts of modern-day Ukraine and Southern Russia.</note> and Scythic <note>Scythic: relating to the Scythians, ancient nomadic people of Central Eurasia.</note> clans, which resided in the forests and wilds of uncultivated Europe, as well as the Egyptians and Phoenicians; had the art of expressing their thoughts by means either of characters or letters, at a very early period; and the assertion of the Irish antiquaries, as before quoted, respecting the learning of the ancient inhabitants of this country, though in a state of nature, may not entirely be void of truth; for the faculties of the human mind, in all ages and nations, are nearly the same, and a state of barbarism (for barbarous the ancient inhabitants of all parts of Europe, before their commerce with the Phoenicians, Greeks and Romans, undoubtedly were) doth not exclude reflection and abstract ideas, from whence arise philosophy and the polite arts.

Egypt is at this time universally considered, by the learned, to have been the source from whence sprang that light of knowledge and learning, which shone with such distinguished lustre in the Greek and Roman empires; so as to furnish the Phoenician traders with letters and learning, sufficient not only to enable them to become the greatest navigators at that time in the world, and thereby to be the means of propagating these useful arts to the remotest nations; a circumstance, as we shall see in the sequel, was really the case; for though from the

--- Page 180 ---
<page-num>170</page-num>
<header>DRUIDISM REVIVED.</header>

Based on the quotations and authorities previously cited, there is the greatest probability that the inhabitants of the western parts of Europe possessed the knowledge of letters long before the Romans were well acquainted with them. However, we should not infer that they possessed such knowledge prior to the era when the Phoenicians—by establishing colonies on the western coasts of Iberia <note>The ancient name for the peninsula containing Spain and Portugal</note>, Gaul <note>Ancient France and parts of the surrounding Low Countries</note>, and the building of Carthage—had extended their commerce to Gaul, Belgium, and the British Isles. This occurred about 250 years before the birth of Christ, and 200 years before the Romans were much acquainted with either Britain or Gaul.

During this period of commerce, it can scarcely be doubted that factories <note>In this historical context, "factories" refers to merchant trading posts or warehouses established in foreign territories</note> might have been established on the different coasts for the greater convenience of trading with the natives for skins, furs, tin, and such other commodities as the respective countries then produced. This would have introduced the knowledge of letters among their philosophers; before which period, it is probable they were entirely ignorant of such alphabetic elements. These authorities, coming from some of the most respected names of antiquity, are extremely favorable to the general tenor of the Irish histories. These histories relate that the ancient inhabitants of this isle received not only their letters but also several of their religious ceremonies from the Milesians <note>In Irish tradition, the Milesians were the final race to settle in Ireland, often identified by early modern historians as coming from Spain</note>, who are supposed to be a colony of Phoenicians or Carthaginians from the western coasts of Spain in a very early period.

Though the Irish chronicles, especially those of the latter ages, are very circumstantial on this subject,

subject, yet as they have not produced any authentic authorities, or living proofs to corroborate their assertions, their histories hitherto have been considered by the learned in general, little better than ingenious fables, the invention of dark and illiterate ages. They do indeed, in several places, specify the names and order of their ancient pagan letters, but have not given the characters themselves, having in their stead inserted the Latin letters of the fifth and sixth centuries; to which they have not only given the names of their pagan, but have also annexed several fanciful interpretations, that have not the least foundation in truth, but arose entirely from the imagination of the respective writers. Notwithstanding therefore the probability, from the authorities before quoted, that the Phoenician <note>Ancient maritime traders from the Levant, often credited in 18th-century scholarship with bringing civilization to the British Isles.</note> and Punic <note>Carthaginian</note> traders did introduce letters both into Ireland and Britain, yet the truth must ever remain involved in darkness and obscurity, and a doubt must ever hang on the assertions relative to the learned state of the pagan Irish, if we were not in possession of living evidence, from several monuments of antiquity, still remaining in different parts of the kingdom; some of which evidently owe their existence to ages prior by some centuries, to the establishment of Christianity in Ireland. The inscriptions found on these monuments confirm, beyond the power of confutation, the assertions of the Irish antiquaries, respecting the literature of the pagan inhabitants of this country.

By these we are empowered to assert, that the Irish druids <note>The high-ranking professional class in ancient Celtic cultures, serving as priests, judges, and lore-keepers.</note> had not only the method of committing their doctrine and learning to writing, but that the

# DRUIDISM REVIVED.

The characters and letters made use of for this purpose bear not only a great affinity to those of the ancient Phoenicians, Carthaginians, and Egyptians, but in several instances are exactly the same; as may be seen on comparing them with the characters and inscriptions on the Bembine <note>The Mensa Isiaca or Bembine Tablet is an elaborate bronze tablet with Egyptianizing motifs, once thought to be a genuine artifact of the cult of Isis.</note> and Rameffæan tables. By these also it appears that the Hibernian <note>Hibernian: a Latinate term for Irish.</note> druids, like the Egyptian priests, made use of both hieroglyphic and alphabetic characters. Their letters also, like those of the ancient Egyptians, were of two species—that is, *sacred* and *profane*; the profane were those used in the common occurrences of life, public contracts, ordinances of state, poems, etc., and mentioned by antiquaries under the denomination of the *Bobeloth characters* <note>Bobeloth: likely a reference to the 'Bobel-Loth' alphabet, a legendary Irish script named after its first two letters.</note>, and were the same or nearly so, with those of the Punic <note>Punic: the language of ancient Carthage.</note> and Phoenician. The sacred were those mentioned under the name of *Ogham* and *Ogham Croabh* <note>Ogham: an Early Medieval alphabet used primarily to write the early Irish language; 'Croabh' means 'branch,' referring to its tree-like appearance.</note>; these letters were mixed with symbols and hieroglyphics in their hiero-grammatic <term>hiero-grammatic: sacred or priestly writing</term> writings, or those which treated of their religion, philosophy, and laws.

The method of describing the human thoughts by the representation of the several objects of nature seems to have been the first effort of the mind towards transmitting the knowledge of past transactions to posterity. At what period therefore this invention took its rise is impossible to determine; it probably was during the infant state of society, as the practice of writing by means of pictures and symbols was universal, not only among all the nations of antiquity, but even such among the modern who are in their first stages of civilization;

### No. 7,
Is a sort of Trellis-work found on one of the tabernacles <note>In this context, "tabernacles" likely refers to the stone chambers or recesses within the passage tomb.</note> at the mount of New-Grange <note>Newgrange is a famous prehistoric monument in County Meath, Ireland, built around 3200 BC.</note>, and on several stones and crosses both in Ireland and Britain. Trellis-work or small lozenges <note>diamond-shaped patterns</note>; amongst the ancient Britons, Germans and indeed all the aboriginals of Europe, signified fate, providence, chance, or fortune. It seems to have been derived from a species of divination used by the Scythic and Celtic tribes, consisting of long quadrangular pieces of wood taken from fruit trees; these pieces had several symbolic characters engraven on them, when, being in the act of divining, they were thrown across; as the several characters fell and answered to each other, the auspices <note>prophetic signs</note> were taken (*c*). These pieces of wood called by the Scythians *run-stobs* <note>original: "run-stobs", likely referring to runic staves</note> and by the Irish *ogham croabh* <note>original: "ogham croabh", meaning "branch Ogham," an early medieval alphabet used for the Irish language</note>, that is, the staves and furrows of wisdom, were retained many centuries after the establishment of Christianity, and their position in divining frequently drawn on crosses and the walls of churches, as emblems of the Divine Providence; nay, the divination itself was retained in some parts of England to the present century (*d*).

In the explanation of Numbers 1, 2, 3 and 4 of the above symbols, it may be objected, that there being no serpents in Ireland, the Irish druids could not have taken that reptile as the symbol of the Divine Being; but we ought to consider that the Celtic religion was not confined to any particular country, but maintained by all the aboriginal inhabitants of Europe; being the religion not only of the ancient Irish, but of the ancient Britons, Gauls, Cimbri, Celto-Iberians, Italians and Thracians; it likewise laid the foundation of that of the Greeks and Romans.

### DRUIDISM REVIVED.

By the few characters hitherto discovered it is extremely probable, that the western Celtic tribes as well as the Irish received their symbols from the Carthaginian and Phoenician traders; though possibly they might have had some sort of hieroglyphic writing in an earlier period; however, it is more than probable, that the use of letters was not known among them long before the Punic <note>Punic refers to the Carthaginians, who were of Phoenician origin.</note> merchants visited their coasts.

The invention of letters or alphabetic characters have by some been placed prior to that of hieroglyphics and symbols; but if we consider, that they were the last effort of the human mind, in order to accomplish an effectual method of transmitting the ideas to future ages, we shall be inclined to place their discovery in much later periods. If they had been the invention of the antediluvians <note>Those living before the Biblical Flood.</note>, or of equal standing with hieroglyphics, as some learned persons have imagined, all the nations of the earth would most probably have been conversant with them, whereas the contrary is sufficiently evinced. All the societies of mankind, in the different periods of time, at a certain stage of civilization, had the method of expressing their thoughts by hieroglyphics if not symbols; but letters were confined among the ancients as among the moderns, to not only a particular, but in a great measure the very same part of the globe. The only nations of antiquity who understood this divine art, were the Egyptians, the Libyans, the Ethiopians, the Arabians, the Indostans <note>People of the Indian subcontinent.</note>, the Medes, the Persians, the Syrians, the Hebrews, the Phoenicians, the Celts, the western Scythians, the Greeks and Romans; compre-

<summary>This page discusses the origins of writing among the Celts, arguing that alphabetic letters were a late human invention likely introduced to Western Europe by Phoenician traders rather than being an ancient or universal art.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the origins of written language, transitioning from a general geographic overview of literacy to a specific focus on Egypt as the cradle of the alphabet.</meta>

# DRUIDISM REVIVED.

<margin>(i) Des Vignoles, Chronology volume 2. Arundel tables. <note>Des Vignoles (1673–1744) was a French chronologist; the Arundel or Parian Marbles are ancient Greek inscriptions.</note></margin>

comprehending the present Europe, the north and north-east parts of Africa, and the south and western parts of Asia; the only part of the world at this day which have the knowledge of alphabet characters, except the European colonies in America and Africa, within the last century.

Egypt therefore, as she was the parent of the several arts and sciences which illuminated the ancient world, probably gave birth to letters; a circumstance not only confirmed by the assertion of several of the Greek and Latin writers (i), but further evinced from the remarkable similarity between all the alphabets of antiquity; for the letters contained in the ancient Ethiopic, Arabic, Malabarian <note>Refers to the Malabar Coast of India.</note>, Chaldaic, Samaritan, Phoenician, Punic, Etruscan, Cuphic <note>Kufic: an early angular form of Arabic script.</note>, Greek and Runic alphabets show evident signs of being derived from one common origin; and several of them, in each alphabet, are actually found as symbols and hieroglyphics, not only in the Ramessaean <note>Relating to the monuments of Ramesses.</note> and Bembine tables <note>The Mensa Isiaca, an elaborate bronze tablet with Isiac symbols once owned by Cardinal Bembo.</note>, but in several inscriptions discovered in the pyramids.

To the Egyptians therefore are we indebted for the invention of letters, which they took from their hieroglyphic and symbolic characters; but the time they made this important discovery cannot be determined; it was probably in a very early period; for Moses, who was skilled in all the learning and wisdom of the Egyptians, wrote in alphabetic characters, which he undoubtedly obtained from thence, though they have been long since lost; the present Hebrew letters not being the

<summary>The author argues that Egypt is the original source of all alphabetic writing, citing similarities between ancient scripts and Egyptian hieroglyphics as evidence. ->DRUIDISM REVIVED.<-

the same in which the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Hebrew Bible, traditionally attributed to Moses.</note> was originally written. But in whatever period Egypt made a discovery so beneficial to the human race in general, it seems to have been the source from which letters proceeded in three distinct channels through the ancient world. The first, taking a northern course, watered the western parts of Asia, and the eastern, southern and midland parts of Europe. The second proceeding westward, enlightened the northern parts of Africa, and in process of time, in conjunction with the first, the western coasts of Europe; whilst the third, being thrown in a south and east direction, furnished the Ethiopians, Arabians and Indians, with the inestimable art. The Egyptian letters, especially during the middle ages of their empire, consisted of three species, that is the literal, the epistolary and hiero-grammatic <note>The author refers to the three scripts of Ancient Egypt: Hieroglyphic, Hieratic, and Demotic.</note>. In the first were written their scientific works, as philosophy and laws; in the second, their common occurrences of life, and in the third, their religious subjects (k). The several people who visited that country, having been instructed in these methods of writing by the priests, introduced them into their own; where they not only were retained for the aforesaid purposes, but from them and the symbolic characters new alphabets were formed, in which the several species of the Egyptian letters were found, causing thereby the letters of the several nations, though derived from a common origin, to be materially different from each other. Moses may be considered the father of the northern branch, and the first who brought letters into

some antique inscriptions, found in Sicily, mentioned by Mr. Brydone in his Tour through that island, and there called the Chaldaic <note>Chaldaic refers to a form of Aramaic, though the author disputes this identification here.</note>; they are not Chaldaic but the ancient Libyan (*r*).<note>(r) The language of these inscriptions appears to be the Punic or Phoenician.</note> The Phoenicians, on their establishment at Carthage under Dido, mixed their letters with the Libyan, whence the Punic alphabets were in several instances different from the ancient Phoenician, and nearly the same as the Massilian <note>Massilian refers to the Greek colony of Massalia, modern-day Marseille.</note>, which had obtained several Libyan letters, from their commerce with these people. From the Massilians the Gauls received their letters, which on the establishment of the Christian religion, being mixed with the Etruscan, laid the foundation of the Latin alphabets of the middle ages. On the conquest of Spain by the Carthaginians, the compound Punic alphabet was introduced into that country; where it seems to have taken place in the commercial affairs of the ancient Iberians, which probably was the same as the Libyan, and that mentioned by Strabo, who says (*s*),<note>(s) Strabo, book 3, page 139.</note> that the priests of the Iberians had the art of writing very early. From Spain, letters probably were introduced, by means of the Iberian and Gallic merchants, some few years before the birth of Christ, into the British isles.

<margin>(u)</margin> in Ireland <note>The author refers to the historical presence of these scripts in Ireland as documented by later chroniclers.</note> (*). The names and order of these letters have been preserved by the latter writers, especially by Ceanfaolidh <note>Cenn Fáelad mac Ailella (died c. 679), an Irish scholar known for his legendary memory.</note>, an author of the seventh century, who is said to have transcribed and illustrated Forchern's grammar; but whether Ceanfaolidh in his transcript made use of Forchern's characters, or those of the Latins then in general use throughout the island, we are not informed; but which ever might have been used for this purpose, the form of the Bobeloth characters <note>A legendary Irish alphabet named after its first two letters, Bobel and Loth.</note> have undoubtedly been lost for several centuries, as none of the writers of the latter ages give the least specimen of them.

As little satisfaction therefore as the bare names and order of letters must be, without the characters themselves, yet they have been the means of supplying the defect and negligence of former antiquaries; and have enabled us, from a number of antient inscriptions found in different parts of the kingdom, and from some MSS. <term>Manuscripts</term> to give the Bobeloth characters complete, as far at least as concerns the alphabet, in the annexed table.

By this table it appears, that the ancient Bobeloth characters of the Irish were all symbols, and bear a great affinity to the Phoenician and Punic <note>Punic refers to the language and culture of ancient Carthage, which evolved from Phoenician.</note>, as will be fully shown in the examination of each particular letter.

The name of the first is *boibel* or *bobel*, which in the old Celtic, Irish and Punic tongues signifies the chief spirit or ghost, from *bo*, *boi* or *boe* a ghost or apparition, and *bel* a chief or lord; from whence *Bal* or *Baal* in the Canaanitish, and *Beal* in the
